Part 2. For Luthier’s maintainer: avenues and new rules

Nov 15, 2026 · @Jason Bryant

Summary

While the engine was offline (14 to 15 November), the songwriting session ran nine research rounds and studies, and wrote two songs in metres other than 4/4: The Room Next Door (6/8) and Sung to Sleep (a 3/4 carol for organ and choir, four editions). Everything is gathered in one file, “Luthier research compendium - 15 November 2026”, and the cumulative fix list (“Luthier fix list since v7”) now runs to 33 findings, 740 to 749.23.

This document does two things:

  • The rules: it states the new rules the research produced, each with its finding number, how strong its evidence is, and how it relates to what Luthier v7 already holds.

  • The avenues: it ranks the avenues the session thinks most likely to make the engine write better music, or judge it more accurately, without human ears.

Three things matter most:

  1. There are now two outside measures of a song’s success, and they disagree. Audiences (YouTube views, 10,847 songs) reward predictable melody, chords and rhythm and dense lines. Musicians (cover counts, 780 and 2,360 songs in two sources) reward harmony that moves within sections and melodies with room in them. Covers and views are unrelated once song age is held fixed. A judge that serves both is the clearest new lever.

  2. Several of the session’s findings confirm rules Luthier already has rather than adding new ones. The YouTube ‘moments people love’ study is listener evidence for Window 2’s designed silence and re-entry. The psychoacoustic checks restate Module 9.14. Those findings are marked as confirmations, so they shouldn’t be ported twice.

  3. Song shape is real but weak. Songs share one build to a late high point, with no types and almost no link to success. Treat it as a craft check, not a judge.

Evidence grades used below: A replicated in two sources or at large scale with controls; B one large study with controls; C suggestive (small, or not controlled); D design rule from theory or practice, untested.

The new rules

# Rule Finding Grade Relation to Luthier v7
1 Metre as a parameter. Bar length, beat grid and metrical weights come from the metre. Kept tables per metre come from the metre atlas (Essen, Lieder, EWLD, Open Hymnal, SymbTr; licence tagged). Additive metres are 2+3 groupings learned from onset weights. 749.4– 749.7, 749.15 A (tables measured; groupings recovered from data) New: Luthier is 4/4- only
2 Hymn mode and choral output.Hymn rhythm patterns and quartiles; a descant module; organ score and hymn page from one timeline. 749.11– 749.13 B Extends Module 11.19 (hymnal page)
# Rule Finding Grade Relation to Luthier v7
3 Accompaniment by section role.Give each section a role and a texture for each hand, measured against the Lieder accompaniment tables: onsets a beat, complementarity, textures. Bass first in free sections; vary repeats that share printed bars by registration. 749.14, 749.16 B (organ practice plus measured tables; song 53 third and fourth editions) New for keyboard and organ; the band arrangement modules are untouched
4 One song-wide intensity curve.The high point falls at about 0.65 to 0.85 of the song as performed, with a short release after it; each return rises above the one before. Score departure from the build, not shape types: there are none. Give shape little weight in the judge. 749.17 A for the shared build (three collections); C for success (under 0.5% of variance) Refines the energy arc (9.12) with measured targets
5 Surprise against expectation in the judge.Melody, chord and rhythm surprise measured against a large popular corpus. Melody at or below the corpus median; spend surprise where the context is settled. 749.18 A (10,847 songs; holds within artist) Confirms Window 67 (precision) and Cheung; adds measured targets and the direction of the effect on views
# Rule Finding Grade Relation to Luthier v7
6 A breath, then a fresh re-entry, at the best moments.Thin the melody for a beat or two before the moment, re- enter on a less expected note, and place the emotional moment at about 0.55 to 0.60 of the song, before the loudest arrival. 749.19 B (8,960 listener- marked moments, 1,546 matched to beats) Confirms Window 2 (designed silence, re-entry) and Window 3 (phrases of 5 to 8 seconds) with listener data
7 The rhythm layer. Rhythm surprise at or below the corpus median for the sung line; pickups into phrases; moderate syncopation; not too sparse in pop (about one note a beat or more); rhythmic surprise at the marked moments. 749.20 A (holds within artist) Adds rhythm to the expectancy judge; agrees with the groove windows (Witek)
8 Perception checks for organ and choir.Port Module 9.14 to organ and choir textures. The pedal is written in its own octave. Count the accompaniment above the sung tune (at most 2%). Measure phrase length in seconds: 2 to 6 is ideal, 8 at most. 749.21 D for the limits (arrangers’ tables); A for top-voice salience Restates Module 9.14 and 9.13; only the port and the seconds measure are new
# Rule Finding Grade Relation to Luthier v7
9 A two-channel judge. Score candidates on an audience profile (rules 5 and 7, denser lines) and a musician profile: harmony that moves within sections (chord repetition below the corpus median in at least one section), melodic room (rests, varied note lengths), conventional chord choices. Prefer candidates good on both. Where they disagree, the sung line follows the audience profile and the harmony and accompaniment follow the musician profile. 749.22 A for harmonic motion (two sources); B for melodic room (one source) New: fills the ‘cover counts’ channel the spec names as missing (Windows 95 and 10.18)

Rule 10, added later on 15 November (749.23): chromatic harmony is a style option, not a default. Keep the departure budget for most songs: in 10,749 songs, more chord time outside the key goes with fewer views, within artist too (grade B), and in 2,355 songs it does not go with more covers. For styles that live in chromatic harmony (gospel, jazz, prog, art song), offer a chromatic home chosen at intake: a section declares its chromatic chords, each costs 1 unit on first entry and nothing after. Prefer applied dominants that resolve; they carry no views penalty once the other note measures are held, and lean positive for covers (grade C). Each style keeps its own norm: in hymn mode, applied dominants are ordinary (about 2% to 4% of chord changes in psalm and chorale settings) and should cost nothing; chorales and art songs live in chromatic harmony (chromatic chords in about half of 8-bar spans; art songs about three times the popular share); popular styles keep the budget. Relation to v7: refines the Departure Cost Framework (Window 19.9) the way the synthetic modal homes already do.

One caution applies to every rule built on views or covers: these are associations in observational data. Fame, exposure and age were controlled as well as the data allow, but not fully. Each effect is a few percent of variance at most. They are tie-breakers and targets, not laws.

The most fruitful avenues, ranked

1. A two-channel ‘will it last’ judge, built at scale

This is the strongest lever for judging without human ears. The session matched cover counts to 780 songs (SHS100K) and 2,360 songs (Discogs-VI), and views to 10,847. Three steps would take it further:

Use all of Discogs-VI (348,796 groups of versions, CC BY-SA 4.0, on Zenodo, with first release years). Join it to every melody source available, not just Hooktheory: Lakh MIDI melodies matched to song titles, POP909, PDMX lead sheets and the Rock Corpus.

  • Add a third channel for hymns: Hymnary.org instances across editions (retention over time). The 5,000-tune export is on disk; the site blocks automated access, so a full export would have to come from the writer’s own download.

  • Replicate on LargeSHS (218,407 works) when its data is published. The address in its paper did not exist on 15 November.

The result is two fitted profiles that every candidate is scored on. Validate them by checking that kept songs outrank their own damaged versions on both, as Luthier’s judge tests already do.

2. Matched versions written by the engine

The literature lacks controlled tests: the same song changed in one respect only. The engine can write them. One song in versions that differ only in:

shape (early, late or flat peak);

  • the re-entry note (plain or fresh);

  • melodic density;

  • harmonic motion (looped or moving chords);

  • the breath before the best moment.

Score every version on both channel profiles and on the perception checks. That tests whether the judges respond to the manipulations as the observational data say they should, and it builds the set any later listener test would need.

3. The moments people love, at scale and over whole songs

The session used one day’s YouTube allowance: 4,000 videos and 8,960 moments, 1,546 of them inside timed clips. Hooktheory clips cover sections, not songs. Two extensions would help:

  • More data: spread the comment collection over several days (about 10,000 videos a day) for five to ten times the moments.

  • Whole songs: align moments to whole-song symbolic transcriptions (Lakh MIDI matched to the same videos), so the best moments can be placed across a whole song.

It is the only data source that marks where in a song people were moved, at scale.

4. A better expectation model inside generation

The session’s surprise model is an IDyOM-style chain (orders 0 to 3) with separate pitch, chord and rhythm viewpoints, used only to judge. Three upgrades would help:

  • Combine the viewpoints: pitch with rhythm, and scale degree over chord, with both long-term and short-term models, as IDyOM does.

  • Train on a larger corpus: Hooktheory plus Lakh melodies.

  • Use it inside the melody search: a target band for surprise in each part of the song, with planned spikes at the marked moments, rather than a filter applied afterwards.

5. Metres beyond 4/4, with outcomes

The atlas gives tables for every common metre, but no outcomes. Hooktheory holds 969 clips in 3/4, 1,266 in 6/4 and 47 in 6/8; PDMX holds many more triple-metre scores with views and ratings. Rerunning the surprise, rhythm and covers models within triple and compound metres would show whether the audience and musician profiles hold there, which is exactly where the engine is heading.

6. PDMX as the large test bed for the perception checks

PDMX has 254,000 public-domain multi-part scores with views, favourites and ratings, and the session already parsed 11,266 of them. Running the roughness, masking and voice-separation checks on the multi-part scores, against those outcomes, would test whether the perception rules relate to anything people do. No study has tested masking of a sung line against song outcomes.

Avenues not worth repeating

  • A deep neuroscience pass. Luthier v7 already holds the core of it: ITPRA, Salimpoor’s two dopamine moments, Koelsch, Vuust and Friston’s precision weighting, Sloboda’s chill map, groove and entrainment, heart-rate syncing, oxytocin and inter-brain syncing in group singing, and Huron’s “Tone and Voice” in Module 9.14. The gaps left are small and hard to act on: the neural resonance account of beat, which preferences are universal (the Tsimane studies), and individual differences such as musical anhedonia.

  • Shape types. Songs do not fall into Vonnegut-style types, tested against null models in hymns, Billboard songs and MuseScore scores. Clustering finds shapes even in random curves (Cornelissen et al. 2026). Don’t build a shape menu.

  • Predicting hits from shape. Shape explained under 0.5% of views or favourites in 11,266 scores, and a non-significant 2.5% to 3.3% of Billboard chart outcomes.

  • Audio from YouTube. Downloading audio with yt-dlp (as Suno did) circumvents YouTube’s protections and breaks its terms. The engine also works from notes, so it would gain little. The Data API’s metadata and comment timestamps are the legitimate route, and they were enough.

  • The Free Music Archive’s play counts. They have no curves over time, and the full audio is too large to use.

Data, code, licences and cautions

  • Non-commercial: CoCoPops Billboard (CC BY-NC 4.0); Hooktheory via Sheet Sage, SymbTr and Essen (CC BY-NC-SA).

  • Other terms: SHS100K is SecondHandSongs metadata (used from the published research list, not the site). Hymnary.org counts are kept as derived figures only.

YouTube statistics and comments are kept only as results, under YouTube’s API terms. The writer’s API key is held outside the repository.

Cautions:

  • Views measure exposure as much as music.

  • Hooktheory clips are user transcriptions of sections, not whole songs.

  • The covers sources list only songs with at least one other version. They compare songs covered more with songs covered less.

Every effect is a few percent of variance at most.

The rules above should tune and break ties; they should not override the engine’s keptsong judges.

Part 3. Metre atlas: a head start for Luthier beyond 4/4

Words and music for the songs: Jason Bryant. Prepared 15 November 2026 while the engine was offline, for 749.4, 749.5, 749.9 and 749.11.

What this is

Kept tables for every time signature the public corpora can support, a test of which tables carry across metres, English stress-to-beat tables from 301 hymns, four-part motion tables from the same hymns, research notes on the metres the corpora barely hold (5/8, 5/4, 7/8, aksak 9/8), and a proposed port. Everything is in the repository under tables/metre_atlas (JSON per metre and source; hymns.json; comparison.json) with the scripts that made it.

The six findings

  1. Pitch carries across metres; rhythm does not. Across nine sung metres the medians of repeated notes, steps, leaps, turns, range and longest run barely move; every rhythm reading moves a great deal, even in beat units. One pitch table can serve every metre; rhythm tables must be per metre (749.4, now on nine metres, not two).

  2. The felt metre matters, not the notation. 6/4 and 6/8 read almost the same in beat units (1.88 against 2.00 notes a beat): they can share a table with a scale factor. 2/2 and 2/4 are close in density (1.81 against 1.64 notes a beat) but divide the beat differently, on a small 2/2 sample (62 tunes). 3/2 and 4/2 are not slow 3/4 and 4/4: they are a plainer, older style (0.74 notes a half-note beat; 44% of 3/2 bars are a whole note and a half). 3/8 is felt in one (2.6 notes a dotted-quarter beat), not as a fast 3/4.

  3. 9/8 and 12/8 can be built from 6/8, beat by beat. In the dance tunes, 9/8 and 6/8 behave alike per dotted-quarter beat (2.55 against 2.68 notes a beat; 82% against 86% of moves a third of a beat). There are almost no sung 9/8 or 12/8 tunes (6 and 0), so sung 6/8’s per-beat tables, extended to three or four beats, are the best available.

  4. Vocal tables must come from sung tunes. The dance tunes are 1.1 to 2 times denser per beat, range 17 to 19 semitones (sung: 12 to 13) and repeat notes far less (6% to 12%; sung: 17% to 22%).

  5. English stress lands on strong beats, by metre (749.5). In 301 hymns’ stanzas (about 40,000 words scored with the CMU Pronouncing Dictionary): in 3/4, 81% of stressed syllables fall on the downbeat; in 4/4, 51% on beat 1 and 38% on beat 3; in 6/8, 84% on the two main beats. Unstressed syllables go the other way.

  6. Real four-part hymn settings move with the tune more than song 52’s lines did (749.9). Altos move with the soprano 35% to 39% of the time, against it 13% to 19%, and hold 44% to 47%; basses move against it most (34% to 40%). Parallel fifths and octaves occur about once in a hundred moves. About 7% to 10% of inner-voice notes are shorter than the beat: passing notes the carol sketch never had.

Sources

  • Sung tunes: the Essen folk-song collection in music21’s corpus (9 metres with 60 or more tunes; 5/8, 5/4, 7/8 and 9/8 with 7 or fewer).

  • Instrumental tunes: music21’s Irish and Scottish dance collections (Ryan’s Mammoth, O’Neill’s 1850, Aird’s Airs, miscFolk), kept apart.

  • Hymns: the Open Hymnal Project (public domain; 301 hymns, four voices, English stanzas under the soprano), read with a parser written for its interleaved ABC (hymn_parse.py).

  • Research (below) for odd metres and text-setting.

Every metre at a glance

Grid in twelfths of a quarter (an eighth = 6). Felt beats: simple metres on the denominator’s note (half notes in 2/2, 3/2, 4/2); compound metres (3/8, 6/8, 9/8, 12/8, 6/4) on the dotted note.

2/4 instrumental 486 24 0, 12 3.21 29% (0, 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, 18, 21) 30%; (0, 6, 12, 18) 14%; (0, 3, 6, 9, 12, 18, 21) 7%
Time signature Source Tunes Bar (twelfths of a quarter) Beats Notes a beat (median) Onsets on a beat Top bar rhythms (onsets; share of bars)

3/4 instrumental 207 36 0, 12, 24 1.30 66% (0, 12, 24) 21%; (0, 24, 30) 6%; (0, 12, 24, 30) 6%

Hymn tunes against folk tunes (749.11)

Hymn tunes in 3/4 (46 hymns), quartiles: notes a bar 2.12 / 2.34 / 2.66; distinct bar rhythms 16% / 22% / 32% (sung folk 3/4: 35% / 47% / 67%); gaps of half a beat 0% / 7% / 24%; steps 49% / 57% / 62%. Song 53’s carol sketch read 1.97 notes a bar (under the hymn range), 16% distinct bar rhythms (the hymn 25th percentile) and no half-beat gaps:

plainer than a typical hymn, as the writer judged. A hymn mode should be charged against these hymn quartiles, not the folk ones. Commonest 3/4 hymn bars: half and quarter (36%), three quarters (26%), dotted half (13%), dotted quarter, eighth and quarter (6%).

The metres the corpora barely hold: research

  • 5/4: five quarter-note beats, usually grouped 3+2 or 2+3; Brubeck’s “Take Five” is 3+2, and the original Mission: Impossible theme keeps the 3+2 subgrouping. Essen has 4 sung tunes in 5/4.

  • 5/8, 7/8 and aksak: additive metres built from short (2) and long (3) pulses. 7/8 is most often 2+2+3 (the Bulgarian rachenitsa), also 3+2+2 and 2+3+2; Balkan 9/8 is usually 2+2+2+3, unlike the Western compound 3+3+3; 11/16 (kopanitsa) is 2+2+3+2+2. Essen has 7 tunes in 5/8 and 2 in 7/8.

  • Text-setting: composers align stressed syllables with strong beats (Palmer and Kelly 1992, on English song); Halle and Lerdahl’s generative textsetting model (Current Musicology 55) was the first explicit model; Hayes treats a good setting as the best resolution of conflicting goals (stress on strong positions, avoiding long gaps between syllables, avoiding too many syllables crowded together, phrase ends at line ends), and published 640 lines of English set to rhythm by native speakers (Hayes and Kaun 1996), a ready test set for the engine’s stress rule. Alignment also holds in French (Temperley and Temperley 2013).

Proposed port (749.4)

  1. A metre object: name; bar length in twelfths; beat groups as a list of lengths (3/4: [12, 12, 12]; 6/8: [18, 18]; 7/8 as 2+2+3: [12, 12, 18]; 5/4 as 3+2: [36, 24]); levels (downbeat, group starts, beats, subdivisions); the felt beat.

  2. Tables by felt metre: shared pitch tables; rhythm tables per felt metre from sung tunes (this atlas); 6/4 from 6/8 by scale (2/2 from 2/4 only with care); 9/8 and 12/8 from 6/8 per beat; 3/2 and 4/2 as their own (plainer) style; additive metres from their groups, each group read as a short (2) or long (3) beat, until a corpus is found.

  3. Readings in beat units (notes a beat, gaps of half, a third, a beat or more), so the judge’s charge means the same in every metre.

  4. The stress rule per metre from the hymn table (finding 5), tested on Hayes and Kaun’s lines.

  5. Harmony and accompaniment per metre: four-part motion from finding 6; bass and accompaniment figures by beat groups (749.6, 749.14).

  6. A hymn mode charged against the hymn quartiles (749.11).

Data still missing

  • Sung English tunes beyond hymns, with words under the notes: DALI (pop songs, notes and lyrics aligned to audio) and the lyrics-melody MIDI set of Yu and others (2021) exist; neither was fetched here.

  • Sung 9/8, 12/8, 5/8, 5/4 and 7/8 tunes in numbers: none in the corpora used.

Files

tables/metre_atlas/: one JSON per metre and source (readings and quartiles, bar rhythms, two-bar windows, intervals, onset weights, durations); hymns.json (hymn readings by metre, stress tables, four-part motion and texture); comparison.json; atlas_build.py, hymn_parse.py, hymn_atlas.py, compare.py.

Round 2 (15 November): the new collections

After a round of deep research (report: “Time signature research and song data”), three more collections were fetched and tabled, every table now tagged with its licence: OpenScore Lieder (1,462 art songs with words, 342 in English; CC0), OpenEWLD (502 popular-song lead sheets, 447 in English; MIT) and SymbTr (2,200 Turkish makam pieces, about 1,550 sung with a syllable on each note; CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, non-commercial). Tables: lieder_mxl.json, ewld_mxl.json, symbtr_usul.json.

Additive cycles from SymbTr: the groupings measured from the notes

The files give the bar length (9/8) but not the grouping (2+2+2+3). Every chain of 2- and 3- unit groups that fills the bar was scored by how much more often notes begin at its group starts than inside its groups. The best chain recovers the grouping the literature gives wherever the literature gives one:

For the engine: the grouping of an additive metre can be learned from a corpus’s onset weights, so the metre object’s beat groups (port, item 1) can be measured rather than typed in. Turkish makam melodies are denser and more ornamented than Western songs; use these tables for structure (groupings, onset weights), and Western sung tables for density where they exist.

  • Non-commercial only: SymbTr (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, share-alike on derived tables); the Essen tables of round 1 (music21’s copy carries permission for non-commercial use only); the dance collections (commercial use unconfirmed).

  • Not reachable from here: the Finnish folk-tune archive (499 tunes in 5/4, MIDI, noncommercial research); the site is blocked from this workspace. A download attached by hand would let the 5/4 table be built.

  • Still empty: sung 11/8 and 13/8 (1 and 4 pieces in SymbTr).

Part 4. Time signature research and song data (research report)

SymbTr and Lieder fill Luthier’s metre gaps

Two open corpora cover most of what Luthier is missing, and no single source covers all of it. OpenScore Lieder (CC0, per-note lyrics on 1,351 of 1,352 scores) supplies the sung compound metres: 50 scores containing 9/8 and 30 containing 12/8, plus 297 English-language songs that take English text-setting well beyond hymns (OpenScore/Lieder). SymbTr v2.4.3, the Turkish makam corpus, supplies the sung additive metres with one lyric syllable per note: about 318 lyric-bearing pieces in 9/8 (mostly aksak), 47 in 5/8, 26 in 7/8 plus 18 in 7/4, and 187 in 10/8. Its licence, though, is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (github.com/MTG/SymbTr). The Finnish folk-tune archive is the only large pool of 5/4 (499 tunes), but it has no aligned lyrics and is licensed for non-commercial research only (Catafolk index). 11/8 and 13/8 have no usable sung corpus anywhere: SymbTr holds 1 and 4 pieces. Those metres have to be built from grouping rules rather than learned from counts. On the literature side, three findings matter most for generation. First, additive metres are sequences of 2- and 3-pulse beats with stable, tradition-specific positions for the long beat. Second, compound metres differ from their simple twins (6/8 vs 3/4, 12/8 vs 6/8) more in phrase-level grouping than in within-beat rhythm. Third, text-setting rules depend on language more than on metre: English aligns stress to beat strictly, French mainly at line ends, Bulgarian folk song hardly at all, and Finnish runosong by syllable length. The team’s own hymn measurements in 3/4 and 6/8 are among the few triple/compound English stress figures that exist, because the published corpora restricted themselves to 4/4.

Corpus weights confirm the textbook hierarchy, but only 4/4 has been measured

The one fully recovered numeric onset profile is the Huron and Ommen tally of 75,242 onsets in Essen 4/4 songs, printed by Murphy (Murphy 2020, Journal MusMat). The downbeat takes 25.5% of onsets. Beat 3 takes 19.0%, beat 4 17.9% and beat 2 13.6%. Eighth off-beats take 2.8 to 7.9%, and sixteenth positions under 1%. Two details are useful for checking Luthier’s own Essen-derived 4/4 table. Off-beat eighths after beats 2 and 4 are about 2.5 times as common as those after beats 1 and 3. The sixteenth just before a beat is 15 to 30 times as common as the sixteenth just after it, which is the dotted-eighth-plus-sixteenth signature; the reverse “Scotch snap” figure is nearly absent from this German-dominated sample. Haydn string quartets rank the 16 positions almost identically: only 2 of 120 pairwise orderings differ (Murphy 2020). That gives a concrete benchmark for “how close is close enough” when the engine compares generated onset histograms with corpus ones. Palmer and Krumhansl (1990) plotted equivalent profiles for 2/4, 3/4, 4/4 and 6/8 from classical keyboard music, but this is known only through a secondary summary and no numbers were recovered (London, Polak & Jacoby 2016). No published numeric profile for 3/4, 6/8, 3/8, 9/8, 12/8, 6/4 or 3/2 was found. The team’s measured Essen weights for those metres are therefore ahead of the accessible literature, not behind it.

The hierarchy does not hold everywhere. In 913 notated Turkish makam pieces, onset histograms did not match the usul accent patterns. In Malian jembe music, a four-beat ternary cycle “analogous to 12/8” (about 42,297 onsets from 15 performances), on-beat positions other than the downbeat are articulated less often than the upbeats just before them (London, Polak & Jacoby 2016). For Luthier this means a 12/8 blues or gospel profile cannot be inherited from 6/8 European folk song and assumed correct. No corpus statistics for sung 12/8 blues, gospel or doo-wop were found at all.

Two findings separate compound metres from their simple twins. In Dutch folk tunes, melodies of the same tune family notated in 6/8 and in 3/4 fell to very low similarity ranks (100 to 128). The authors attribute this to “a very different rhythmic structure expressed by a different meter notation” (Volk et al., ISMIR 2007). In other words, transcribers’ choice between 3+3 and 2+2+2 reflects real rhythm, and the engine is right to keep separate tables for 6/8 and 3/4. The other side of this is that jigs (6/8) and slides (12/8) are confused with each other in Irish audio classification, while compound tunes show autocorrelation peaks at multiples of three subdivisions (Beauguitte, Duggan & Kelleher). The inference is that 12/8 vs 6/8 is mostly a matter of bar and cadence placement over four dotted-quarter beats rather than a different within-beat vocabulary. Temperley and Temperley’s level scheme gives a ready way to assign strength: the tactus (level 2) is the quarter in 2/4, 3/4 and 4/4 and the dotted quarter in 6/8, 9/8 and 12/8. Level 3 is the bar in 2/4, 3/4, 6/8 and 9/8 but the half-bar in 4/4 and 12/8, and level 4 is the bar in 4/4 and 12/8 (Temperley & Temperley 2013).

London’s theory adds one design rule. A time signature is not a fixed felt metre: the same notated structure is heard differently at different tempi, hence his “tempo-metrical types” and “many meters hypothesis” (London, Hearing in Time, introduction). Luthier should key each table on time signature plus tempo band. Fast 3/8 is felt as one beat per bar. 6/4 (2×3) and 3/2 (3×2) share a bar length but not a grouping. This is theory-derived; no study comparing listener perception or corpus rhythm for 3/8 vs 3/4 or 6/4 vs 3/2 was retrieved. For a realistic performance ceiling, metre-inference models trained on Essen recover the notated metre about 67 to 71% of the time at Markov orders 1 to 4 (van der Weij, Pearce & Honing 2017). That figure is a sensible target if the engine tests whether its generated melodies “sound like” the metre they claim.

Additive metres are chains of 2s and 3s whose long beat lands

The Balkan and Turkish additive metres all sit on a fast, roughly equal pulse grouped into short (2-pulse) and long (3-pulse) beats. The standard forms are 5 = 2+3 or 3+2, 7 = 2+2+3 or 3+2+2, 9 = 2+2+2+3, 11 = 2+2+3+2+2 or 2+2+2+2+3, and 13 = 2+2+2+3+2+2 and variants (Wikipedia, “Aksak”). The placement of the long beat is fixed by dance type, not free. Hristov’s 1913 Bulgarian theory, now translated, says paidushka (5 = 2+3) puts the elongated part last with a “comparatively stronger” accent, and that the reverse order “is not observed in songs”. Rŭchenitsa (7 = 2+2+3) is long-last, while makedonsko horo puts the long beat first (3+2+2) (Hristov, trans. Goldberg, MTO 30.4). Singer’s Macedonian work and Rice’s Bulgarian work document 3+2+2 and 2+2+3. Fracile’s survey of about 6,000 transcriptions lists those two but not 2+3+2, so a Balkan-style generator should essentially never emit 2+3+2 in 7/8. This rests on Clayton’s summary, not the original (Clayton, MTO 26.1). London’s well-formedness constraints, also known here only through Clayton, give the encodable model: an isochronous pulse level, beats drawn from {2, 3}, maximal evenness (spread the 3s out among the 2s), beat rates of 400 to 1200 ms, and cycles no longer than about 2 s (Clayton, MTO 26.1).

The timing of short and long beats matters only at render time, but the measurements disagree enough to state ranges rather than a single ratio. Theory gives 2:3, a long/short ratio of 1.5. Goldberg timed 18 tŭpan recordings by 9 players and found rŭchenitsa and elenino horo within about 10 ms of 2:3, with a worst case of 23 ms. Group-initial beats ran slightly long and mid-group beats slightly short, and in Pirin rŭchenitsa the first bar of each two-bar pair was 19 ms longer than the second (Goldberg, GMTH 2015). Bernacki’s two Pirin-Macedonian 3+2+2 songs gave beat proportions of 38:29:33 and 44:26:30 against a theoretical 42:29:29. That works out to long/short ratios of about 1.24 and 1.59, with the first short beat the shortest in over 80% of cycles; he concludes these “cannot be described by a 2:3 beat ratio” (Bernacki 2023). Bernacki’s result rests on two YouTube recordings, so it shows that variation exists rather than how often. A defensible humanisation band is long/short 1.2 to 1.7, centred on 1.5, with symbolic durations kept as exact 2- and 3-pulse values. Tempo is bound to the type: rŭchenitsa’s first short beat runs about 245 bpm (a two-bar unit near 35 bpm), and elenino horo’s first beat about 138 bpm (Goldberg, GMTH 2015). Rŭchenitsa short beats therefore fall below London’s 400 ms beat floor. For phrasing the felt beat is the 2/3 group, but at that speed listeners track the whole bar or two-bar unit.

The melodic evidence is thin. Heavy right-hand (düm) strokes mark group onsets in both Bulgarian and Turkish accompaniment (CompMusic usul examples). In the related North Indian 3+2+2 cycle rūpak, beats 3, 5 and 7 are subdivided more than 95% of the time, but the long first beat only 28% (42 recordings), so the long group often carries one held note (Clayton, MTO 26.1). No corpus study of melodic onset density by pulse position in Balkan or Turkish aksak was found. SymbTr’s validated bar lines would allow the team to measure this directly, and that would be a genuinely new result.

Perception argues for redundancy. North American adults failed to detect disruptions in 2+2+3 Balkan tunes that Bulgarian and Macedonian adults, and 6- to 7-month-old North American infants, detected (EurekAlert summary of Hannon & Trehub 2005). Western-only adults also missed metre violations in complex Turkish metres while catching them in simple ones (Kalender, Trehub & Schellenberg 2013). The immigrant-adult group numbered only 17 and the Hannon and Trehub figures come from a press release, so treat this as directional. For a Western audience, Luthier should keep one grouping throughout a song, put melodic onsets on group starts and avoid mid-song regrouping unless it is a deliberate effect.

Lyric stress follows language rules more than metre rules

English sets stress to strong beats strictly, everywhere in the line. Palmer and Kelly found compound nouns took the stronger metrical accent on their first syllable in 43 of 48 Gilbert and Sullivan cases and 96 of 105 art-song cases, about 90%. Adjective-noun phrases were set far less reliably (83% in one corpus, 42% in the other), so lexical stress is near-hard and phrasal stress soft (Palmer & Kelly 1992). Hayes and Kaun’s English folk-song data show matching tightening toward boundaries (“beginnings free, endings strict”). Only about 1.1% of intonational-phrase endings mismatched, against 3.9% at phonological-phrase ends. Some 10.9% of settings were “headless”, leaving opening positions empty as an anacrusis or rest (Hayes & Kaun 1996). Hayes’s constraint grammar ranks avoiding lapses (three empty grid positions in a row) above keeping stressless syllables off strong positions. It reproduced the consultants’ favourite setting on 70.6% of lines against 23.1% for Halle and Lerdahl’s algorithm (Hayes 2009). The paper reports 364 lines and Hayes’s web page 346; the source has a typo somewhere. The size of the language effect is measurable. English stress-meter alignment (SMAV) is 1.15 against 0.77 for French. Yet 97% of French line-final syllables are stressable, and without them French alignment drops to 0.49 (Temperley & Temperley 2013). The English comparison set is only ten songs.

Style shifts English rules sharply. In 80 rock songs, 33.3% of stressed syllables are syncopated. Most of them (77% at eighth level) anticipate beats 1 and 3 by an eighth, and the rate rises with tempo and year. The 19th-century English song comparison set had none (0 of 602) (Tan, Lustig & Temperley 2019). In 679 pop lead sheets, downbeats and half-beats strongly favour stressed syllables, and 55% of primary-stress notes are longer than the song’s mean note (Nichols et al. 2009). Both studies analysed only 4/4 (and 2/4). Hayes and Kaun forced binary chanting, and Temperley and Temperley reported no compound-metre breakdown. That leaves no published quantitative study of English text-setting in 3/4, 6/8, 9/8 or 12/8. The team’s hymn figures (81% of stressed syllables on the 3/4 downbeat, 84% on the two main beats of 6/8) are therefore a primary measurement, not a replication. The triple and compound rules Luthier uses (one syllable per eighth for anapests and dactyls in 6/8; stressed-quarter plus unstressed-eighth for iambs and trochees in 6/8) are extrapolations from binary grid models that the Lieder English subset can now test.

Outside English the governing variable changes. Bulgarian folk verse counts syllables and ignores stress, even though Bulgarian stress is contrastive. Of 89 coded songs, 50 use 8-syllable lines with a fixed 5+3 caesura in 34 of them, and the same text can be sung in several aksak metres: lesno 7/8, rŭchenitsa 7/8, pajdushko 5/8, dajchovo 9/8 or kopanitsa 11/8 (Hayes & Schuh, Bulgarian metrics handout). This is a small, teaching-handout sample. Luthier should map Bulgarian-style lines to whole bars (for example two bars of 7/8 per 8-syllable line, with the caesura on a bar or beat boundary) rather than forcing stress onto the long beat. Finnish Kalevala metre is governed by syllable length. Stressed syllables must be long on the downbeat and short on the upbeat, with strictness rising through the line until foot 4 has no violations (Kiparsky 1968). Sung runosong is most often in 5/4 or 4/4 (Finnish Music Quarterly), and Archangel Karelian solo singing mostly used a “five-beat, two-line melody” (Kallio 2017). The exact 8-syllables-into-5-beats template (lengthening the final syllables) comes from a non-peer-reviewed university page and remains unverified against Launis’s transcriptions (UAF). In Ottoman song, Bektaş’s study of 466 beste found that usul correlates with classes of quantitative aruz metre rather than individual metres (Bektaş 2005). For SymbTr lyric tables, that means syllable weight (long vs short) is the property to track against beat position, not stress.

Ranked datasets for the missing metres and English lyrics

The table ranks sources by how much they fill the stated gaps: sung 9/8, 12/8, 5/8, 5/4, 7/8 and 11/8, and English lyric-aligned songs beyond hymns. Most counts were computed by the research team from cloned repositories on 2026-11-15 and are not published figures. “Any” means the score contains that signature anywhere.

12 Dick Atlee Balkan page about 35 tunes in 5, 7, 9, 11, 15, 18, 22 counts with explicit q/s groupings Not stated; mostly dance tunes ABC, MIDI None stated; many links broken dickatlee.com/music/balkan

Mapped onto the gaps, the picture is uneven. Sung compound 9/8 and 12/8 with lyrics come almost entirely from Lieder (50 and 30 scores, English 12 and 5). That is enough for onset tables in German plus English, but thin for English-only stress tables in 12/8. The Lieder 9/8 scores are presumably compound triple (3+3+3), but the grouping was not checked. Sung 9/8 aksak is SymbTr’s strongest suit, at about 284 vocal aksak pieces plus smaller usuls written in 9/8 (evfer, çifte sofyan, raks aksağı, oynak, aydın). The usul row gives only the cycle length, not the 2+2+2+3 grouping, so the grouping must come from an external usul table or be inferred from onset density (github.com/MTG/SymbTr). These smaller usuls’ groupings were not verified and should not be assumed to be 2+2+2+3. 5/8 has about 50 lyric-bearing türk aksağı pieces in SymbTr and 63 unlyricised Finnish tunes. 5/4 is Finnish (499) with negligible lyric-aligned support (Lieder 4, SymbTr 3). 7/8 has about 44 SymbTr vocal pieces (devr-i hindî and devr-i turan, both 7/8 and 7/4), 14 Finnish tunes plus a few with explicit additive signatures, and 3 Lieder scores. 11/8 and 13/8 are effectively empty (SymbTr 1 and 4, Finnish 2 in 11/8). English lyric-aligned songs beyond hymns come to roughly 760 aligned scores from Lieder EN (297) and OpenEWLD (464), with PDMX as the route to thousands more once filtered.

Several promising-sounding sources should be rejected. The Session’s data dump now forbids processing “with Large Language Models”, including “utilizing LLM tools”, and it is instrumental anyway (TheSession-data LICENSE). Yu et al.’s 265,020 syllable-note pairs from Lakh MIDI carry no bar or time-signature field, so they cannot feed per-metre tables (yy1lab repo). DALI (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0) and the HookTheory release (CC BY-NC-SA 3.0) are audio-aligned and lack score-level metre (DALI; Donahue et al.). The Sacred Harp datasets are metadata only, although their time-signature column shows 6/4 plus 3/2 at about 11% of the 2025 edition (45 + 23 of 590), a useful prior for hymn-style triple metres (edjw/Sacred-Harp-datasets). Lanphier’s Bulgarian songbook is PDF and MP3 only. The Persian Radif corpus deliberately excludes metric pieces (arXiv 2507.10456). No open, lyric-aligned symbolic corpus of Bulgarian, Macedonian, Greek (beyond the Aegean set), Romanian, Serbian or Irish sung song was found.

Licence terms decide which tables Luthier can keep

Only three of the useful sources are clean for any use: OpenScore Lieder (CC0), OpenEWLD (MIT) and the Greek Aegean set (CC BY 4.0). PDMX is CC BY 4.0 at the record level, but 12.29% of its songs have conflicting licence metadata, and the paper itself labels the corpus both “CC-0 / Public Domain” and CC-BY (Zenodo 15571083; arXiv 2409.10831). Any PDMX subset should be restricted to the “no_license_conflict” rows and filtered on the per-song licence column.

The additive-metre sources are all encumbered. SymbTr’s README specifies CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, while Zenodo lists “Other (Open)” and the CompMusic site footer says CC BY-NC-ND 3.0 (SymbTr README; CompMusic corpora). The safest reading is the most specific one, non-commercial and share-alike. The Finnish archive is “available for research purposes (that is, noncommercial)” (esavelmat download page). The MTC features are CC BY-NC-SA 3.0. Whether derived statistical tables (onset weights, syllable-to-beat probabilities) count as adaptations under NC-SA is a legal grey area the research did not resolve. If the ZenEng web app is or becomes commercial, tables derived from SymbTr, the Finnish archive or MTC need either permission from the rights-holders or a clean-room rebuild from CC0 and public-domain material.

Two licence issues also touch work the team has already done. The full Essen release carries the CCARH licence: “for personal use (single-user) only”, “only for the purpose of academic research”, and no “derivative editions intended for commercial distribution” (license.txt). The research did not check music21’s own redistribution terms for its bundled Essen subset, so the team should confirm whether its existing Essen-derived tables inherit this restriction. The Open Hymnal GitHub mirror carries no licence file (mzealey/openhymnal). The hymns themselves are largely public domain, but the encodings’ terms were not verified because openhymnal.org was unreachable. Creighton, Child Ballads, Erk, the Hayes data files and the Atlee ABC page state no licence at all.

What Luthier should encode, with evidence grades

The table condenses the findings into encodable defaults. “Measured” means a corpus or performance figure from a cited source. “Team” means the team’s own existing measurements. “Inferred” means a design extrapolation from theory or neighbouring data, which needs testing against the datasets above.

Metre Beat template Weight and timing defaults Text-setting default Evidence
3/4 3 quarter beats Downbeat strongest; table already measured from Essen Stress on downbeat (team 81%); two-syllable feet either fill beats 1–2 and hold, or stress-as-half-note with beat 3 as pickup Team; settings inferred
3/8 1 felt beat per bar at fast tempo Bind to a tempo band; treat 2- and 4-bar units as hypermetre One stress per bar Inferred (London theory)
3/2 vs 6/4 3×2 vs 2×3 Keep separate grouping templates; 6/4 + 3/2 about 11% of Sacred Harp Hymn-style, as 3/4 and 6/8 respectively Measured prior; templates inferred
6/8 2 dotted-quarter beats, each 3 eighths Distinct from 3/4 (Dutch IMA result); tactus = dotted quarter Stress on the two main beats (team 84%); anapests and dactyls one syllable per eighth; iambs and trochees long-short Team + measured; syllable patterns inferred
9/8 compound 3 dotted-quarter beats Build from 6/8 beat-level patterns; learn from Lieder As 6/8, three stresses per bar Inferred; data in Lieder (50)
12/8 4 dotted-quarter beats; level 3 = half-bar Mark through cadence and bar placement, not new within-beat rhythms; blues/gospel profile genre-specific and unmeasured As 6/8, strongest stresses on beats 1 and 3 Inferred; data in Lieder (30)
5/8, 7/8, 9/8 aksak, 11/8, 13/8 5 = 2+3; 7 = 2+2+3 or 3+2+2 (never 2+3+2 in Balkan style); 9 = 2+2+2+3; 11 = 2+2+3+2+2; 13 = 2+2+2+3+2+2 Downbeat > long-beat onset > other group onsets > inner pulses; phrase-final long note on the long beat; plan in two-bar units; playback long/short 1.2–1.7 English: stress on group onsets, phrase stress on downbeat or long beat. Bulgarian style: syllable count to whole bars, stress free. Turkish style: long aruz syllables to longer notes Groupings measured/secondary; weights and text rules inferred
5/4 (Kalevala) 5 quarter beats over an 8-syllable trochaic line Final syllables lengthened to fill beats 4–5 Long stressed syllables on strong beats; strict in feet 3–4, free in foot 1 Rule A measured; rhythmic template inferred
All, English style shift — Rock: allow about one-third of stresses to anticipate beats 1 and 3 by an eighth; hymn and 19th-century style: none No lapses of three empty positions; lexical stress near-hard (about 90%), phrasal stress soft Measured (4/4 only)

Several of the cited figures rest on small or secondary foundations and should be weighted accordingly. Small samples: Bernacki’s 2 recordings; Goldberg’s 18; Temperley and Temperley’s 10-song English set; the 80-song rock corpus; Palmer and Kelly’s 10- and 12-singer performance studies; Hayes and Schuh’s 89 Bulgarian songs; Hannon and Trehub’s 17 immigrant adults. Secondary sources: London’s NI-metre constraints and Fracile’s 7/8 frequencies (both via Clayton), the aksak grouping table (Wikipedia), Palmer and Krumhansl’s profiles (via London et al.), Hannon and Trehub (via a press release), and the Finnish 5/4 template (a university web page). Internal count conflicts: Hayes and Kaun’s corpus is 670 lines in the paper but 640 in the data file, and the two Essen counting passes disagree slightly on additive files (for example 6 vs 8 files in 5/8). Both passes agree that Essen holds fewer than a dozen files in any additive metre.

Conclusion

The gap list splits into three tiers, each needing a different method. Compound 9/8 and 12/8 can be learned from Lieder under CC0, and the English subset there finally lets the team test its extrapolated 6/8 and 12/8 syllable rules against real settings rather than hymns alone. 5/8, 7/8 and 9/8 aksak can be learned only from Turkish data under a non-commercial licence, which makes SymbTr ideal for research tables and risky for a shipped product. 11/8, 13/8 and English-language additive songs cannot be learned at all from existing corpora. For those, the right design is generative: a London-style grouping grammar (pulses, {2,3} beats, maximal evenness, tempo bands), with weights and text-setting transferred from the measured 5, 7 and 9 tables.

The most valuable next measurements are ones nobody has published. These are per-pulse onset density in SymbTr’s vocal aksak and türk aksağı pieces, which would show whether the long beat really carries the held note, and stressed-syllable placement by beat in Lieder’s English 6/8, 9/8 and 12/8 songs, which would extend the team’s hymn figures to secular song. Both are feasible with data already identified here. Either would put Luthier’s tables ahead of the literature, which has measured English stress alignment only in 4/4 and has never counted melodic onsets in aksak song.

Part 5. Accompaniment corpora and music theory (research report)

Plan the verses, not just the notes

Luthier’s organ and keyboard parts sound too simple mainly because every stanza gets the same treatment, not because any one bar has too few notes. Fixing the density matters too. Two kinds of evidence point the same way. Organ pedagogy from PCUSA, Reformed Worship and RSCM Australia agrees on a plan: keep inner stanzas close to hymnal harmony but vary the texture and registration, and save rich harmony for introductions, interludes and a unison final stanza with free accompaniment, often after a modulation and sometimes with a descant (PCUSA Quicksheet; Reformed Worship, Brink). On the computational side, the systems that work best treat accompaniment texture as a chord-independent pattern. They retrieve or generate it phrase by phrase, keep it consistent across sections with the same label, and vary it between section types (Wang et al. 2020; Whole-song generation). On density, the engine’s own measurements show the current carol organ part runs at about 40% of the onset rate of 19th-century Lieder piano parts in 3/4, with a quarter of their rhythmic complementarity to the voice. For triple and compound metres, the only open accompaniment data with melody and accompaniment kept apart are OpenScore Lieder (CC0; 265 songs that open in 3/4 and 216 in 6/8) and the Open Hymnal (305 SATB hymns, 48 in 3/4). Every major learned accompaniment system filters its training data to 2/4 and 4/4. No open symbolic corpus of contemporary worship, musical theatre or film accompaniment exists. Of the music-as-language theories, only expectation models (IDyOM and its relatives) have direct evidence linking their scores to listener pleasure. Grammars, schemata and GTTM work well as generators and structural checks, but nothing yet shows that they work as quality judges.

The carol organ plays at about 40% of the Lieder piano’s rate

The engine’s own Lieder accompaniment tables give a baseline for 3/4 piano writing. The median right hand makes 1.53 onsets a beat (IQR 1.13–1.95). The median left hand makes 1.17 (0.88–1.51). 65% of accompaniment activity complements the voice rhythmically (50–73%). Textures are split among mixed (29%), broken (27%) and block (26%), with melodic (7%), sustained (7%) and waltz (4%) figures well behind. In 6/8, broken chords lead at 35% and the right hand rises to 2.4 onsets a beat. Against this baseline, the engine’s measurements of the current carol organ part in verses 1–3 are RH 0.63, LH 0.45 and complementarity 15%. That is about 1.9 right-hand attacks and 1.35 bass notes per 3/4 bar: a held chord with an occasional change. The right hand sits 44% below the bottom of the Lieder interquartile range, and complementarity is less than a third of the Lieder lower quartile.

There is a strong counter-argument. Lieder piano writing is the wrong benchmark for congregational stanzas. Every organ source reviewed wants the inner stanzas of a hymn left close to the hymnal harmony, so that basses can sing their part and “the primary sound must come from the congregation” (Reformed Worship, Brink). Hymnal harmony moves with the voices, so low complementarity in those stanzas is idiomatic, not a fault. The numbers still show a real problem. If the carol tune moves mostly in quarter notes, as most 3/4 carols do, plain hymnal doubling would put the right hand at about one onset a beat. At 0.63 the organ is sustaining across tune notes, which makes it thinner than the hymnal itself. (This is an inference from the engine’s figures, not a measured comparison against the tune.) The bigger problem is what the measurement implies about the other stanzas. If verses 1–3 all score alike, the part has no plan across the verses, and the final verse is where organists put the free, fuller writing. The fix therefore has two layers. Bring congregational stanzas up to at least hymnal-doubling density and vary their texture. Then write designated “free” stanzas, interludes and piano-accompanied songs to Lieder-like density and complementarity bands.

Two cross-checks support the engine’s Lieder texture mix. In the only rigorous texture-annotated keyboard corpus, oscillating figures (Alberti and rocking patterns) appear in only about 5.1% of bar labels even in early Mozart (Couturier, Bigo & Levé, ISMIR 2022). That matches the engine’s 4% waltz share. Stereotyped figures are seasoning in real repertoire, and an engine that defaults to oom-pah-pah in every 3/4 song, or Alberti bass in every ballad, over-represents them relative to the styles it imitates. The same corpus also shows that accompaniment layers are present in 84.4% of bars in slow movements but only 58.2% in faster ones. Reflective, slow songs should almost always carry an explicit harmonic layer rather than leaving the melody exposed.

Only Lieder and the hymnals supply open triple-metre accompaniment

The research team cloned the main repositories on 2026-11-15 and counted time signatures directly, so most counts below are team measurements, not published figures. OpenScore Lieder is the strongest single source. It holds 1,352 MuseScore scores of 19th-century voice and piano under CC0. By first time signature there are 265 in 3/4, 216 in 6/8, 33 in 9/8 and 20 in 12/8. Counting a signature anywhere in the score gives 332 with 3/4, 247 with 6/8, 50 with 9/8 and 30 with 12/8, and 248 scores change metre. The corpus also ships 1,156 Roman-numeral analyses (.rntxt), so the accompaniment can be read against labelled harmony without running a chord detector (OpenScore Lieder). The Open Hymnal Project is the only open hymn corpus with full parts: 305 ABC files, 286 of them in four voices. By first metre, 203 are in 4/4, 48 in 3/4, 10 in 6/4, 7 in 3/2 and 6 in 6/8. It has no licence file; headers say the hymns must be “public domain or freely distributable” (mzealey/openhymnal). It supplies the “hymnal harmony” baseline but none of the free accompaniment the engine is missing. Hymnary.org offers standard public-domain harmonisations as PDF and MusicXML, with no terms stated for those downloads (Hymnary.org, OLD 100TH).

Modern pop accompaniment is cleanly available but is almost all 4/4. POP909 (MIT licence) gives each of 909 Chinese pop songs an identical three-track layout of MELODY, BRIDGE (secondary melody) and PIANO accompaniment (POP909 repo). Downbeat files give 880 songs in 4/4 and 29 with six-beat bars, which matches a 2026 paper’s count of “29 in 3/4 or 6/8” (arXiv 2602.15074). The metadata spreadsheet implies about 52 non-duple songs instead, a conflict the team could not resolve. MIDI time-signature events are unusable: 617 of the 909 files claim 1/4. PDMX is the largest openly licensed multi-part score pool. It holds more than 250,000 MuseScore scores under a CC BY 4.0 umbrella, with track, genre and lyric columns. However, 12.29% of songs carry conflicting internal licences, and user claims that modern songs are public domain cannot be trusted, so only the 222,856-song “no licence conflict” subset is defensible (Zenodo PDMX v9). PDMX is the only realistic route to modern worship, theatre and film piano arrangements in 3/4 and 6/8. Its metre distribution was not counted because Zenodo blocked shell downloads. Lakh MIDI (176,581 files, CC BY 4.0) is a large multi-track pool of uneven quality (LMD). Pop1K7 (1,747 pop piano covers, CC BY 4.0) is entirely 4/4 and keeps melody and accompaniment in one part (Zenodo 13167761).

The gaps matter as much as the holdings. The team found no open symbolic corpus of contemporary worship (CCM), Broadway piano-vocal scores or film reductions, and no annotated corpus of pop, gospel or worship piano textures such as worship pads over octave left hands. Classic free-accompaniment collections exist only as scans, and most are in copyright in the US. Bairstow’s Organ Accompaniments to 24 Unison Hymn-Tune Verses (1941) is public domain except in the US. Coleman’s Varied Hymn Accompaniments (1953, sixteen tunes with three settings each) is public domain only in life+50 countries. Thiman’s 1937 set is not public domain anywhere (IMSLP Bairstow; IMSLP Coleman; IMSLP Thiman). These collections share tunes with the hymnals (OLD HUNDREDTH, ST ANNE, HYFRYDOL, RICHMOND), so optical music recognition of the permissible scans would produce paired “hymnal versus free” settings of the same melody. No such paired corpus exists anywhere.

Good accompaniment builds by section, fills the voice’s gaps and saves its surprises

Texture is layered, and the two-hand split is not enough

The Algomus texture syntax is the best available vocabulary for describing what an accompaniment does. Each bar is labelled as stacked layers: M (melodic), H (harmonic) and S (static, for pedals and ostinati). Each layer has a voice count and figure flags for homorhythm, parallel motion, octaves, sustained notes, repeated notes, oscillation and scales (ISMIR 2022). A label such as M1/HS1b means one melodic voice over a single persistent oscillating accompaniment voice. The most common full label in early Mozart is melody over one accompaniment voice (7.3%), and two-layer, two-voice bars make up 29.2% of configurations. The authors conclude that a plain left-hand/right-hand split “is not representative” of real texture. The reliability of the scheme is unproven: no inter-annotator agreement statistic exists, and reviewers flagged 31% of the labels, of which 22% were changed. Classical string quartets show the same melody-over-accompaniment dominance, at about 74% of 691 segments (Giraud et al., ISMIR 2014). Pedagogy adds the pop catalogue: arpeggios over an octave bass, block-chord rhythms such as the “1 (2) &” pattern in “Thinking Out Loud”, and repeated eighth- or quarter-note chords that run from Schubert’s “Gute Nacht” to Sara Bareilles. It also notes that bass and top voice a tenth apart is a characteristically sonorous spacing (Puget Sound, Arpeggiated Accompaniments; Block Chord Accompaniments).

Density rises through the song and repeats by section label

Professional pop arrangements build. In the POP909 example analysed by the whole-song generation team, verses use mostly eighth notes and choruses sixteenths. Fill figures appear at phrase boundaries, and activity and pitch height rise in the second half toward a climax (Wang, Min & Xia, ICLR 2024). This evidence is qualitative; nobody has published density curves by section. Repetition by section label is measured. The ratio of accompaniment similarity within the same phrase type to similarity across types (ILS) is 1.68 ± 0.09 in human arrangements, against 1.19 for the best generator. Every chorus sounds like every other chorus, and choruses sound unlike verses. Listener research on pop choruses agrees: choruses are higher, brighter and more compressed in dynamics, while harmonic variance does not separate them from verses (Van Balen et al., ISMIR 2013).

Complementarity is real but uncalibrated outside the engine’s own tables

The evidence that accompaniment should move where the voice does not is directional, not numeric. In Bach’s two-part Inventions, the fusing perfect consonances arrive asynchronously more often than synchronously (1,059 against 599 occurrences), while imperfect consonances tend to arrive together (1,901 against 832). Avoiding simultaneous onsets on fused intervals keeps the parts perceptually separate (Huron 2008). Learned arrangers trained on POP909 insert a secondary melody when the lead rests, which reproduces the professional BRIDGE track (Wang et al. 2020). No published study gives a target complementarity value, melody-to-accompaniment density ratio or register gap for song accompaniment. The engine’s own Lieder figure of 65% (50–73%) is therefore ahead of the literature. It is the best calibration target available for piano-accompanied song.

Hymn and organ practice puts its richness at the edges

The organ sources agree with each other more than any other body of evidence in this report, though it is practitioner consensus rather than measurement. Every source says the text sets the treatment. RSCM Australia’s Steven Nisbet writes that “the meaning of the text dictates the mood of the hymn’s accompaniment and level of harmonic complexity” (RSCM Australia, Nisbet). PCUSA’s guidance says to “reserve richer harmonies for introductions, interludes, or final verses” and adds that “simplicity is often more effective than complexity” (PCUSA Quicksheet). Emily Brink warns against playing every stanza the same way, and against the over-loud organ that most often turns congregations against alternative arrangements (Reformed Worship, Brink). For inner stanzas, a single organ-training course (one author’s view) gives a concrete texture menu: the melody soloed on one manual with alto and tenor on another and bass in the pedals, the tenor soloed, the alto raised an octave as a near-descant, soprano and tenor swapped, manuals only for a reflective verse, and pedal points before cadences or in interludes (Organ Training Course, Lesson 10).

The final stanza follows a well-documented recipe. Everyone sings the melody in unison, and the organ part is fuller, with lower pedal notes, “more secondary dominants (especially over chromatic bass lines)” and often five or more notes per chord (Wikipedia, Last verse harmonisation). Nisbet’s working method is the clearest algorithm in the sources. Fix the melody, write the bass next, then fill the inner parts, and aim for a continuous progression rather than isolated “spiky” chords. He offers five bass archetypes with tune examples: tonic pedal (SONG 34), dominant pedal (SLANE, NICAEA, LASST UNS ERFREUEN), descending line (NUN DANKET, CRIMOND), ascending line (ANTIOCH, ELLACOMBE) and walking bass (BATTLE HYMN). His governing rule is: “when you go off into new harmonic territory you have to come back safely” (RSCM Australia, Nisbet). PCUSA’s vocabulary list adds passing diminished chords, suspensions, modal interchange, gospel harmony and ii–V, with tritone substitution used “sparingly”. A descant needs harmony written to fit it, or “the descant melody may clash” (Wikipedia). The King’s College recordings show descants arriving in stages over 1954–1963 and drawing complaints of a “perfect rainstorm of descants”, so a descant is optional, not a default (Williams, York thesis). Willcocks’s last verse of “O come, all ye faithful” opens with the melody in three octaves, the lowest in the pedals, before the harmony departs from the standard setting (Wikipedia).

Modulation before the last verse has clear harmonic rules but no fixed length. Thomas Read says to weaken the old key before establishing the new one, and to precede the new dominant with its own applied dominant or diminished seventh so it does not sound like a half cadence in some other key. For a half-step lift in major, where the two keys share no chords, a German augmented sixth is “one of the quickest and simplest” routes (The Diapason, Read 2022). The AGO’s examined method for a modulating bridge is to think in the target hymn’s metre, take a motif from its opening, restate it a fourth or fifth away, arrive, and make a clean break before the singers enter (AGO, Gehring 2013). Jan Overduin’s only limit is that the interlude be “no longer than the hymn itself”. He prefers the half step as “more liberating” than the whole tone, but that is one author’s taste against the common modern whole-step lift (Reformed Worship, Overduin). No study measures whether any of these practices improves congregational singing. The engine is encoding expert craft, not tested effects.

Working systems separate texture from chords and choose by phrase

The productive line of accompaniment research comes from NYU Shanghai’s Music X Lab, and its central move transfers directly to a rule-based engine. Wang et al. split piano music into two latent factors. Chords are a beat-wise 36-dimensional vector (root, bass, chroma). Texture is a piano-roll “shape” that deliberately carries minimal chord information. A texture can then be re-voiced onto new chords while keeping its left-hand pattern and contour (Wang et al. 2020). AccoMontage retrieves whole phrases of real accompaniment from POP909 and selects a path through them by Viterbi dynamic programming. Node scores measure rhythm and chord fit to the query melody. Edge scores combine learned texture similarity between adjacent phrases with a “form” term that rewards matching phrase labels. The chosen texture is then re-harmonised to the new chords. Users can pre-filter by rhythm density and voice count (low, medium, high). In a 72-person listening test it beat template-only and sequence-to-sequence baselines on coherence and structure (p < 0.05) (AccoMontage). AccoMontage2 adds harmonisation by dynamic programming over 5,762 curated chord-progression templates, scored at three levels: note-level melody–root dissonance, phrase-template matching and whole-piece coherence (AccoMontage2). Whole-song generation cascades four levels: form (keys and phrase labels), reduced lead sheet, lead sheet, then accompaniment. That ordering is the one an engine should follow (Whole-song generation).

Evaluation in this field is weak but usable. Systems report overlap of pitch and duration distributions with real data (Polyffusion: 0.89 and 0.96), chord accuracy, degree of arrangement, and the ILS structure ratio. The ground-truth targets are DOA 0.333 and structure score 1.980, against 0.300 and 1.520 for the best multi-track system (Polyffusion; Structured arrangement v4). Listener panels are small, from 23 to 72 people. Every one of these systems is trained on data filtered to 2/4 and 4/4. The whole-song model allows three beats per bar but calls 3/4 “underrepresented” in POP909, and no system was trained or tested on hymn, gospel, worship or organ textures. For Luthier the lesson is to copy the architecture, not the data. Store texture templates as an onset grid plus chord-tone indices plus register. Author or harvest the 3/4 and 6/8 templates from Lieder, because no learned model will supply them. Choose templates phrase by phrase with a fit-plus-transition-plus-form score. Map each template onto every chord as it arrives.

Expectation models can judge; grammars and schemata can only generate and check

The expectation family is both implemented and validated. IDyOM, written in Common Lisp, gives per-note information content (surprise) and entropy (uncertainty) from symbolic sequences. Its short-term model learns only from the current piece and its long-term model from a training corpus (IDyOM wiki). It runs from Python through the peer-reviewed py2lispIDyOM wrapper (py2lispIDyOM), and Harrison’s ppm R package implements the same core method without Lisp (pmcharrison/ppm). The key validation used about 80,000 chords from 745 Billboard songs stripped of melody and lyrics. Pleasure depended on the interaction of uncertainty and surprise: listeners enjoyed surprising chords when the context was predictable, and predictable chords when the context was uncertain. The interaction also showed in the amygdala, hippocampus and auditory cortex (Cambridge summary of Cheung et al. 2019; MPI CBS). A 2025 study reports an inverted-U relation between predictability and preference, but it was read only through a secondary summary (MindBlog). This finding lines up closely with hymn practice. By the final stanza the tune is fully predictable, because the congregation has just sung it several times. A reharmonised last verse therefore puts surprising chords exactly where the uncertainty-surprise interaction says they please most, and the “come back safely” rule restores predictability at the cadences. IDyOM’s short-term/long-term split could measure this directly: train the short-term model on stanzas 1–3, then score the free stanza’s chords against it. That is the report’s inference, not a published test.

Effect sizes argue for using these measures as filters, not oracles. In the Hooked! study (1,715 song segments, 973 participants), recognisability tracked conventionality and melodic repetition. But the features explained a marginal R² of only 0.10 at best, against 0.47 once song identity was included (Van Balen et al., ISMIR 2015). On the Billboard Hot 100, each extra chorus repetition raised the odds of reaching No. 1 by 14.5%, a finding read through a secondary source (Pacific Standard summary of Nunes et al.).

The grammar and schema theories are formal and implemented but have no evidence on liking. Rohrmeier’s jazz-harmony grammar models prolongation and preparation as nested dependencies (EPFL DCML). Harasim’s probabilistic abstract grammar, trained on 357 iRealPro standards, recovers expert trees 45.95% of the time against 15.35% for random trees (ISMIR 2018). That shows it can find structure; it says nothing about quality, and the model is jazz-specific. The deep-learning GTTM analyser deepGTTM-IV produced correct time-span trees for 29 of 30 pieces, but no current public download was confirmed (Zenodo 10114163). Voice-leading schema detection in Mozart reaches F = 0.894 on balanced data but precision near 0.01 on real, unbalanced data. A naive detector would find galant schemata everywhere, so schema checks must be tied to known positions such as phrase openings and cadences (Finkensiep et al., ISMIR 2020). Style priors matter. In the Rock Corpus, IV→I (1,162) outnumbers V→I (788), and ♭VII is common (de Clercq & Temperley 2011). A common-practice “dominant must resolve” grammar would therefore wrongly penalise idiomatic worship and pop accompaniment, though it suits hymn harmony. Nobile’s telos principle (verse as initiation, prechorus as build-up, chorus onset as arrival) gives a form check for where the accompaniment should lift. It rests on close analysis rather than a corpus (Nobile, MTO). Patel’s shared-syntax hypothesis is contested: language regions respond weakly to music, and people with severe aphasia judged melodic well-formedness normally (Fedorenko lab preprint). “Music as language” is a productive engineering metaphor, not a licence to equate parse probability with beauty.

Ranked rules for Luthier to encode

The ranking weighs how much each rule addresses “too simple”, how strong its evidence is, and how cheaply a rule-based engine can implement it. “Consensus” means practitioner agreement across two or more independent bodies. “Engine” means the engine’s own measurements. “Inferred” means a design step derived from cited material but not tested.

Rank Rule Concrete encoding Evidence
1 Stanza-role plan for hymns and carols Intro (first and last phrase, or refrain) → st. 1 hymnal harmony → inner stanzas each with a different texture from a menu (melody solo, tenor solo, alto up an octave, manuals only, pedal point), chosen by text mood → optional lighter penultimate → optional 2–4-bar interlude → final stanza unison melody with free accompaniment → ending over a pedal point Consensus (PCUSA, Brink, Nisbet, Wikipedia); texture menu single-author
2 Density bands by role, not one setting Congregational stanzas: RH at least the tune’s own onset rate, 4 voices. Free stanzas, interludes and piano songs: 3/4 RH 1.13–1.95, LH 0.88–1.51, complementarity 50–73%; 6/8 RH about 2.4 a beat, broken texture leading. Rise in activity and register into the last verse or chorus Engine (Lieder tables); build curve qualitative (POP909)
3 Texture templates separate from chords Store each template as onset grid × chord-tone index × register, labelled in Algomus syntax (M1/H3h, M1/HS1b); write native 3/4 and 6/8 templates harvested from Lieder; cap stereotyped figures (waltz, Alberti, rocking) at about 5% of bars Systems (Wang, AccoMontage); Mozart 5.1%; engine 4% waltz
4 Last-verse reharmonisation generator Melody fixed; one bass archetype per phrase (tonic pedal, dominant pedal, stepwise descent or ascent, walking); expanded vocabulary (V/x over chromatic bass, vii°7/x, 9ths, 4–3 and 7–6 suspensions, iv or ♭VI borrowed, relative minor at a phrase start); hymnal cadences kept; 5–6 voices plus pedal; continuous, mostly stepwise motion Consensus (Nisbet, PCUSA, Read, Wikipedia)
5 Complementarity in the voice’s gaps Put fills and secondary-melody fragments where the melody holds or rests, especially at phrase ends; in countermelody layers avoid same-onset arrivals on octaves and fifths with the tune Directional (Huron 2008; Wang 2020; POP909)
6 Phrase-level selection with transition cost Choose a template per phrase by Viterbi: fit to melody rhythm and chords + similarity to the neighbouring phrase + matching section label; keep ILS-style ratio near 1.7 (same-label similar, different labels contrasting); in strophic hymns, deliberately break same-label repetition across stanzas Systems (AccoMontage; whole-song ILS 1.68)
7 Modulation interlude module Interrupt or extend final tonic; quote the opening motif in the hymn’s metre; weaken the old key; whole step via V/V or ii–V of the new key; half step via German sixth respelled as the new V7; applied dominant or vii°7 before the new V with 4–3; rest or held V7 before entry; never longer than one stanza Consensus (Read, Gehring/AGO, Overduin); chord spellings inferred
8 Expectation judge for reharmonised stanzas Score chord information content with IDyOM or ppm, short-term model trained on earlier stanzas; reward surprise mid-phrase where entropy is low, require low surprise at cadences; reject globally extreme means Strong for the interaction (Cheung et al. 2019); inverted U secondary; stanza application inferred
9 Descant as an option tied to rewritten harmony Offer only on the final stanza; regenerate the harmony against the descant; never on by default Wikipedia; King’s history (Williams thesis)
10 Style-specific harmony priors and form lift Hymn and carol: common-practice voice leading and V→I cadences. Worship and pop piano: Rock-Corpus priors (IV→I, ♭VII); accompaniment lift at the chorus onset (telos) Rock Corpus counts; Nobile (analytic)
11 Voice-leading constraints switched by texture Block pads may move homorhythmically; independent layers avoid parallel perfects with the tune; accompaniment top voice below the melody; wide spacing low, close high; bass–top tenths favoured in arpeggios Weak (Huron 2001 abstract only; pedagogy)
12 Do not use as quality judges GTTM trees, grammar parse probability and note-level schema detectors: use only as generators or position-tied checks No liking evidence; schema precision about 0.01

Ranked datasets for accompaniment tables

Datasets are ranked by usefulness for building separated-accompaniment tables in 3/4, 6/8 and modern styles, weighted by licence safety. Metre counts were computed by the research team from cloned repositories on 2026-11-15 unless marked otherwise. “First” means the first time signature in the file and “any” means the signature appears anywhere.

Rank Dataset Melody and accompaniment kept apart? Metre counts Size, format Licence How to get
1 OpenScore Lieder Yes: voice + piano staves; 1,156 Roman-numeral analyses first: 4/4 421, 3/4 265, 2/4 237, 6/8 216, 9/8 33, 12/8 20; any: 3/4 332, 6/8 247, 9/8 50, 12/8 30 1,352 .mscx / 1,462 .mxl CC0 1.0 git clone https://github.com/OpenScore/Lieder (repo)
