749.23 chromatic no chord outside the key pop: the departure budget Yes

Arrangement and documents

The record (build_p64.py) holds every event: him, her, the other voices, eight parts. She sings the engine’s line an octave up (chorus 4, the tag’s second line). The bass in the sung sections is the routine’s (bass_v134), root–five elsewhere. The 9.12 arc as read from the demo: verse 1 0.67, chorus 1 0.82, chorus 2 0.90, chorus 3 0.97, turnaround 0.80, verse 4 0.68, chorus 4 0.99, tag 0.79. Stems: the voices +5.2 dB over the band.

Readback: every staff of the lead sheet, the full score and the parts matches the record (him 432, her 62, harmony 45, acoustic 3,246, Telecaster 124, fiddle 56, steel 201, piano 220, bass 711 pitches; drums 1,521 of 1,521 hits). MusicXML: every part’s onsets and the lyric count read back. consistency_p64.py: 70 of 70 checks pass. The producer one-sheet fits one page in the Word copy.

Readings the paper does not give

  • 715/730: the judge’s pick and the pick of record differ only by 749.1; the pick of record’s charge is the run’s least among the eligible.
  • 734: two raises in turn that did not count; the power returned to 1.000 (round 4).
  • 739: printed each run beside its kept middle halves (run log).
  • 438: the chart drawn over the plan: verse 0.56 changes a bar, chorus 0.75; Bm entered the chorus’s line 3; the designs’ half-bars kept (G, D in choruses 1–3; D to G in chorus 4 and the tag).

Findings

749.24 The engine assumes a bridge

Problem. Song 54 has no bridge. Six readers in the v7 file read BAR0["Bridge"] or LINES["Bridge"] and stopped the run: the bridge plan draw, the era reading’s three windows, 278’s aims, 208/279’s bridge-opening gate, the lift’s bridge median and the reading’s bridge axes.

Evidence. The first smoke test failed on each in turn; songs 48–51 all had bridges.

Fix. Make the bridge optional: draw its plan only if it is sung; read the era over the windows present (two here); leave the bridge’s aims and gate unread when there is none.

Check. A song with no bridge runs with no song-specific guard; songs 48–51 reproduce their orders.

749.25 Placement deaths at nine- and ten-syllable lines with borrowed rhythms

Problem. Two-thirds of builds died for want of a placement (667 main, 662 control), the most of any song since v7 (songs 49–51: 741, 747, 749.3).

Evidence. A trace wrapped the rhythm draw and logged every call over 80 control builds. Failed and kept calls had the same room (earliest start and latest last onset alike), so the window is not the cause. The failures fall on lines of 9 and 10 syllables in two-bar slots whose rhythm is borrowed: verse lines sung again on verse 1’s onsets (712), and verse 1 and chorus 1 lines drawn on a partner’s or a returning unit’s bars (310, 361). Of the 58 sung lines, 18 have 9 syllables or more.

Fix. When a borrowed rhythm cannot take a line’s syllables, split its longest note (as 682 does for the template) or draw the bar fresh, rather than end the build.

Check. Song 54’s main run keeps its pick; placement deaths fall under a third; songs 48–51 reproduce.

749.26 Two syllables on one onset, in most of the clean survivors

Problem. 749.1 again, and larger: 87 of 143 clean survivors (61%) sing two syllables on one onset, and the judge’s pick was one of them (“coffee” / “can”, verse 4).

Evidence. Main run; re-pick by 730’s rule over the other 56 (42 eligible), d12c2728e6f1.

Fix. A note of no length is a death (or 715 makes the build ineligible); find where the second onset is lost (a melisma split on the line’s last note, by the look of the case).

Check. No survivor with two syllables on one onset; the pick unchanged where it had none.

749.27 The band-over-the-tune limit does not carry over to a low lead

Problem. 749.21’s limit (the accompaniment above the sung tune at most 2% of the time) was set on organ and choir, where the tune is the top line. With a baritone under guitars, fiddle and steel, 81% of his notes have something above them, as on most country records.

Evidence. Song 54: him 81%, her 15% (her accompaniment was kept below her); within a second of the tune, 13%; the voices sit 5.2 dB over the band.

Fix. Keep the 2% limit where the tune is the top line (choir, organ, a high lead). For a lead below the band, read salience by level and by what lies within a second of the tune.

Check. Songs 52, 53 and 54 each pass under the rule that fits them.

749.28 A story’s turn falls later than the shape targets

Problem. 749.17 puts the high point at 0.65–0.85 of the song and 749.19 the breath at 0.55–0.60. In a story song whose twist is in the last verse, both fall late: the breath at 0.853, the top at chorus 4 (0.857–0.952).

Evidence. Song 54’s form (A), the writer’s; her entry is the payoff and cannot move earlier without changing the story.

Fix. Read the targets against the song’s turn when the type is Narrative: the breath before the turn and the high point at it, wherever the story puts them, with the release after.

Check. A Narrative song’s breath and top fall at its turn; other types keep 0.55–0.60 and 0.65–0.85.

749.29 The lexicon check: dialect forms and “a.m.”

Problem. Five of the six unknown words were g-dropped forms of known words (sleepin’, mornin’, fixin’ and so on), and “Git ‘n’ Split” read its “n” as the letter N, stressed. “a.m.” splits into “a” and “m”. Protocol 0b v67 asks one word at a time; the writer’s rule is one question a turn, so the six went as one question.

Evidence. Song 54’s intake; readings in gen_l7_p64.py (LEX64); “‘n’” added to the function words.

Fix. Derive -in’ forms from the -ing entry; read “‘n’” as unstressed AH0 N; read “a.m.” as one word, EY2 EH1 M; ask only words with no derivable reading, all in one question.

Check. Song 54’s lyric needs only “mildewed” asked.

Blind pick

The three 10.20 slots (q1020.py): remembered, “For a boy who’s been gone too long”; middle, “The man Mama prayed I’d be”; annotated, “And sir I’m finally fixin’ to be”. Offered shuffled as A (annotated), B (remembered), C (middle); the writer chose C, “The man Mama prayed I’d be”: Middle.

Findings

749.30 One-bar lines die in fresh draws

Problem. The chorus’s one-bar frame lines (4 to 6 syllables) carried most of song 55’s placement deaths: 678 a thousand, 590 of them on chorus lines 2 to 4 and their copies.

Evidence. The trace (songs/p65/diag/trace_place.py, 60 control builds): chorus 1 line 4 failed 14 calls of 35 and line 2 11 of 49, drawn fresh (no borrowed rhythm), with the same room in failed and kept calls. The cause is inside the draw for a one-bar slot, the span (600) by the look of it, not the window.

Fix. Read the one-bar draw’s failures by step (span, gaps, run lengths) and let a one-bar line of up to seven syllables take any span that fits the bar.

Check. Song 55’s main run keeps its pick; deaths at the one-bar lines under one in ten.

749.31 A short line spread across two bars with a long rest inside

Problem. Lines of four to six syllables in two-bar slots can be drawn with a rest of three beats or more between their words.

Evidence. The pick of record: “To be plumb (3.5 beats) sick of it” and “Never got (3.5 beats) to”. They pass every reader.

Fix. Cap a line’s inner rest at the kept lines’ 90th percentile for its syllables and slot, or give such lines one bar.

Check. No pick with an inner rest over the kept 90th percentile.

Part 17. Luthier audit: harmony and arrangement

16 November 2026. Words and music: Jason Bryant. The live copy is the Doc Luthier audit: harmony and arrangement.

Summary

The engine writes one thing: the sung melody. On harmony and arrangement, the record (Luthier v7, 15,350 lines, plus its tables) holds a great deal of research but almost no code that writes anything.

  • Harmony. 24 items, among them six measured tables. In code: the chord chart’s redraw (438), the bass’s inversions (440), and the melody’s fit to the chords (375, 386, 431, 432, 441, 442). Missing: anything that writes a section’s chord progression. Every song’s chord plan has been typed by an AI, including song 36’s “chords by the section jobs (177)”, which were a hand-written list.

  • Arrangement. Module 9 (9.1 to 9.14), the rock and metal modules (9-R, 9-M), Windows 118, 119, 143 and 144, and the offline findings 749.14, 749.16 and 749.21. In code: the bass routine (Windows 143 and 144) and the readers (9.12 energy arc, 9.13, 9.14). Missing: drums, the chordal part, instrumental figures, the texture plan and parts playing. Every band part but the bass has been written by an AI, per song, in that song’s build script.

So a v8 that writes everything needs two new generators, harmony and arrangement, built the way the melody is: drawn from tables measured on real songs, and tested. Much of the measuring is done already; the main gaps are sequence data (what follows what) and data by genre and section role.

How to read the status

Status Meaning
Writes The engine’s own code makes these notes or chords, the same way every run, with no AI choosing.
** Reads** The engine’s code measures or checks it (a reading, a gate, a consistency check) but writes nothing.
** Table** The numbers were measured from real music and saved; no code writes from them yet.
** Rule** Written in the spec as what a song should do; applied only by an AI writing each song by hand, if at all.
** Missing** Not in the record.

Evidence is the fix or Window number in the spec, and the file where the code lives (the engine of record is songs/p66/melody_l7_p66.py ; the bass routine bass_v134.py).

Harmony: what the record holds

Of 24 harmony items, the engine writes from four and reads four; the other sixteen are tables, rules or missing, and nothing writes from them. No code writes a chord progression.

Item Where What it says Status
The chart’s changes Window 143, fix 438 A section’s bars with two changes, and bars holding the last chord, inside the kept quartiles (Table ED) ** Writes**: adds or holds changes within a plan it is given
The bass under the chord 440 The root at nine changes in ten; a third or fifth in the bass with a step beside it ** Writes**(inversions, BASS8)
The melody against the chord 375, 386, 431, 432, 441, 442 Chord tones on the first beat near 72%; no note a semitone from a chord’s outside tone ** Writes**the melody’s fit; the chords themselves are given
Modal chord tones in minor 349, 353 A minor song’s measures read on its own tonic; its outside notes at the kept minor rate ** Writes**(melody only)

Harmony by class Window 105, 173 to 175; Table CN Vocabulary, root motion, chords a bar, tonic time, sevenths, inversions in seven bodies of music (24,137 Hooktheory pieces to 54 Mozart sonatas) ** Table** (zeneng_v88_tables.json)
Pull and distance 175, Table CO Each chord’s pull toward the tonic and each move’s distance, by class Table
Section jobs Window 106, 176, 177; Table CP Verse opens on I and holds most tonic; chorus opens on I or IV and adds a chord; bridge and pre-chorus end on V; first and last chords and joins in 739 McGill songs ** Table**; song 36’s “chords by the section jobs” were a hand-typed list

Modulation Window 55 9% of McGill songs modulate, once, mostly up a semitone or tone, near the middle ** Table**; the v7 engine’s lift is switched off (LIFT2 = 0)
Harmonic rhythm Part VII update; type tables (0.05 to 0.40 departure) Rate of change by type; one deceleration before the bridge or last chorus ** Rule**
Mode as colour palette Part IV-D; type tables A mode for each song type (Dorian for Paradox, Ionian or Dorian for Longing) ** Rule**; chosen at intake from options an AI wrote
Chord vocabulary by type Module 0.4, type tables Allowed and forbidden chords by type (min7, sus4 for Paradox) ** Rule**
The deceptive cadence Window 8; item 111 V to vi at a seam in a Longing song ** Rule**
Blues and gospel harmony Windows 15, 16; Modules 21, 22 The twelve-bar system; the vamp in a new key ** Rule**
Hymn setting Window 57; 749.11 to 749.13 Four parts against hymn quartiles; passing notes in inner voices ** Rule**; songs 52 and 53 by AI sketch scripts
Chromatic harmony as a style 749.23 Applied dominants and mixture rates by style ** Table(studies/chromatic) and Rule**
Harmony voices as lines 749.9 A harmony part written as a line, not a shadow of the lead ** Rule**

In every song since v7 the engine wrote the sung melody and an AI wrote the chord plan and every band part but the bass. Read from each song’s generator and build script.

The gaps, and the research that would fill them

The record already describes what real harmony and bands do on average; what it lacks is what follows what, and how it differs by genre and by section. Those are the two things a generator needs.

Rank Gap What to research Data What the engine gets
H1 A section’s chord progression Sequences of chords by section kind and genre: loops, lengths, harmonic rhythm, how a section ends and joins the next McGill Billboard (the raw annotations, not only Table CP); Hooktheory; a large genre-labelled chord collection A progression drawn per section from kept sequences, gated by Tables CN and CP
H2 The order of chords and melody Whether real songwriting and the data favour chords first, melody first, or both together; how well a drawn progression fits a melody already drawn Hooktheory (melody and chords aligned) A decided order of work, and a fit test
H3 Mode and borrowed chords by genre Rates of minor, Dorian, Mixolydian, ♭VII,♭VI and applied dominants in country, soul, rock, worship and pop The same collections, by genre Per-genre vocabularies replacing the hand-picked type tables
H4 Harmony and backing voices When a second voice joins, and at what interval; answers and crowd voices Arranging literature; multitrack vocals where they exist A harmony-voice writer with its own test
A1 Drums by genre and section Grooves, fills and crashes for country, soul, rock and pop, and how they change from verse to chorus Lakh MIDI with genre tags (the existing Table DD, split by genre); style-labelled drum performances A drum writer drawing a section’s pattern and fills from kept bars
A2 Texture by section role Which parts enter, leave, thicken or thin, section by section, in pop records (749.16 has this only for Lieder) Multitrack MIDI with section labels (POP909’s structure; Lakh with sections found by repetition) A texture plan by section role, replacing the hand plans and the 9.8 stack
A3 The chordal part by instrument Comping rhythms, voicings and registers for piano, organ and guitar against the voice, by genre (Table DE pools all instruments) Lakh MIDI, split by instrument and genre A chordal-part writer with Table DE’s vocabulary per instrument
A4 Fills and answers in the voice’s gaps Their length, register and notes by genre (the existing figure table pools them) Lakh MIDI lead tracks A fill writer for the gaps, never under a sung word (379)
A5 The bass at any tempo Bass cells by tempo and subdivision, so a The 434 transcribed patterns, read by tempo The routine made tempo-aware
ballad at 72 does not run sixteenths

Two cautions from the record itself: harmony and arrangement features have never separated hits from other songs (Windows 28, 38, 56), so these tables should describe what real songs typically do, not promise success; and every new table needs a kept-song test, as the melody’s do.

Proposed order of work for v8, and the test

Harmony first, then the band, then a test that makes “the engine writes everything” checkable.

  1. ** Research H1 to H3**, then build the harmony writer. The engine’s order becomes form, harmony, melody, arrangement; the chart redraw (438) and the bass’s inversions (440) stay as they are.

  2. A5 : make the bass routine read the tempo. A small job, and it removes the one place the routine failed.

  3. Research A1 to A4, then build the drum, texture, chordal-part and fill writers, each with its own kept-song test.

  4. H4 : the harmony-voice writer.

  5. The no-hand-input test. A run may take only the writer’s intake answers (from a fixed questionnaire written from the spec’s Q0 to Q8) and his locked lyric. The song’s generator may set only those. The test report lists every input that did not come from the engine, and a song fails if any chord or band note came from a hand-typed list.

  6. Rerun songs 54 to 56 under the test, and compare them by ear with the versions already delivered.

Until then, a delivered song should say plainly what wrote it: the engine’s melody, an AI’s chords and band.

Data on hand and data to fetch

The measured summaries are in the repo; the raw collections they came from are not, and the research needs them again.

Data What it gives Where it stands
zeneng_v88_tables.json Harmony by class and by section; outer voices; cadences In the repo (song folders’ tables)
zeneng_v101_band_returns_eras.json Drums, the chordal part and parts playing in 2,667 band arrangements In the repo
zeneng_v100_bass_and_figures.json Bass lines in 2,165 songs; instrumental figures in 879 In the repo
zeneng_v123, zeneng_v124 The chart and the bass at and between the chords In the repo; used by the engine
accompaniment_lieder.json Accompaniment textures in 1,403 Lieder In the repo (metre atlas)
McGill Billboard annotations Chords in time with section labels, 890 songs To fetch again (raw)
Hooktheory Melody and chords aligned, about 24,000 pieces To fetch again (raw)
Lakh MIDI (clean subset) with genre tags Multitrack band parts by genre To fetch again (raw)
POP909 Pop songs with melody, accompaniment and structure To fetch

Groove MIDI Dataset 13.6 hours, 1,150 files of human drumming in many styles, by 10 drummers (CC BY 4.0) New; to fetch

This session’s shell can reach only package sites and GitHub, so the large collections may need to be attached or fetched where the earlier studies fetched them; each study script’s docstring says how.

Part 18. Harmony research for Luthier v8: what follows what

16 November 2026. Words and music: Jason Bryant. The live copy is the Doc Harmony research for Luthier v8: what follows what.

A chord writer for the engine is feasible from measured sequences: on verses and choruses in major keys, a simple model drawn from 592 training songs already passes as kept on every measure tried. Minor keys, bridges and pre-choruses need more data, genres need data this session cannot reach, and real progressions loop in a way the simple model does not.

The data

Two collections were read in full; the two largest genre-labelled collections could not be reached from this session.

Collection What it is Used for Licence
McGill Billboard 890 chart slots, 739 unique songs charting 1958 to 1991; chords bar by bar with section labels and the tonic; raw files from ChoCo v1.0.0 (Zenodo 7706751) Every measure here, and the prototype CC0
Rolling Stone 200 de Clercq and Temperley’s Roman-numeral analyses of 200 songs, written as section rules The replication Research corpus
Chordonomicon Over 666,000 user-made chord charts with section, genre and date labels Not reached: its download host is refused by this session’s network, and its dataset card says CC BY-NC 4.0 (non-commercial), against the paper’s CC BY 4.0 CC BY-NC 4.0 (card)
de Clercq’s country corpus 200 famed country songs in Nashville numbers Not reached (its site is refused) Unknown

The Billboard songs carry no genre labels, so nothing below is by genre. Mode was read per song: minor when the tonic chord is minor more often than major (128 minor, 611 major).

What the published research says

Pop harmony has its own grammar, not common practice’s: IV and ♭VII matter as much as V, progressions loop, and genre labels are a weak guide.

  • Rock harmony (de Clercq and Temperley 2011). A hand analysis of 100 songs from Rolling Stone’s 500 Greatest Songs, 20 a decade from the 1950s to the 1990s, with over 90% agreement between the two analysts. IV is the most common chord after I and most often precedes it; the five commonest chords are I, IV, V, ♭VII and VI; II, VI and III form one group of chords that go together, and ♭VII, ♭III and ♭VI another, which the authors read as a modal organisation. paper; slides

  • Root motion. The asymmetries of common-practice music (fourths up preferred to fourths down) are mostly absent in rock, so a chord’s place before or after the tonic is the useful distinction. summary

  • The McGill Billboard set (Burgoyne, Wild and Fujinaga 2011). 890 slots sampled at random from the Billboard charts, annotated by experts with sections and chords; released under CC0. paper; licence

  • Loops since 2000. A corpus of 2002 to 2021 songs is more repetitive than McGill’s; most chord loops appear in only one song, and the most schematic loops were shared more widely in the McGill era (Chander and Quinn 2023). preprint Loops of IV, V and vi hovering above the tonic (the “plateau loop”) became widespread in recent pop (Duinker 2019). article In post-2000 EDM-pop the loop is mostly one diatonic collection, with the melody moving independently above it (Park 2025). dissertation

  • Genre. User tags put as much as 60% of recent songs under “pop/rock”, and song-by-song uniqueness makes corpus averages a weak stand-in for any one song. article Country was studied by corpus only recently: de Clercq built a 200-song country corpus to test the “three chords” caricature, which Jocelyn Neal calls a misconception. talk

  • Writing chords by model. MySong (Simon, Morris and Basu 2008) chose chords for a sung melody with a hidden Markov model trained on a song database, and listeners rated the results positively. paper A semi-Markov version adds each chord’s duration, so it finds where the harmony changes as well as what it changes to (Groves 2013). paper Comparative studies judge generated chords by listening tests on fit and interest against the human progression. study

  • Chords first or melody first. Songwriters do both; some start from a progression and let its mood draw out melody and lyric, others hand a melody to a co-writer to harmonise. No study found settles which works better, so the engine should choose by its own test. example

What the Billboard songs show

Each section kind has its own job, and the jobs are sharp enough to write by: verses open on I three times in four, bridges end on V nearly half the time, and pre-choruses hardly touch the tonic.

Major keys (medians; first and last chord: share of sections; bigrams: share of a kind’s chord changes).

Section Sections Bars Changes a bar Distinct chords Tonic’s share Commonest first Commonest last Commonest moves
Verse 1,690 12 0.88 4 41% I 78% I 39%, V 29% IV→I 11%, I→IV 10%, V→I 8%
Pre-chorus 219 6 1.00 4 13% IV 24%, I 23%, vi 16% V 39%, IV 15% IV→V 9%, I→IV 8%
Chorus 1,646 8 1.00 4 33% I 57%, IV 15% I 35%, V 28% IV→I 10%, I→IV 9%, V→I 8%
Bridge 317 8 0.88 4 14% IV 34%, I 15% V 46% IV→I 8%, IV→V 7%
Intro 557 5 1.00 3 50% I 63% I 38%, V 22% IV→I 10%, I→IV 10%
Outro 339 10 1.00 3 50% I 66% I 50% IV→I 12%, I→IV 12%

Minor keys (128 songs).

Section Sections Changes a Distinct Tonic’s Commonest Commonest Commonest
bar share first last moves
Verse 361 0.69 3 48% i 76% i 41%, V 16%, ♭VII 11% i→IV 5%,♭VII→i 5%, i→♭VII 5%
Pre- 66 1.13 5 17% ♭VI 32%, i ♭VII 21%, V ♭VI→♭VII 10%
chorus 29% 20%
Chorus 367 1.00 3 31% i 54% i 23%,♭VII 17%, V 14% ♭VII→i 6%, ♭VI→♭VII 6%, i→♭VI 5%
Bridge 53 0.88 4 24% ♭VI 26% i 23%, V 21% ♭VI→♭VII 8%

What else the songs show:

  • Chords from outside the key. In major, ♭VII is in 18% of verses and choruses and 22% of bridges; II (V of V) in 12% to 22%. In minor, a major IV, Dorian’s raised sixth, is in 23% of verses and 24% of choruses, the commonest chord from outside the key.

  • The joins. A pre-chorus or bridge hands to the chorus on V→I most often (24% and 36% of joins); a chorus hands to a bridge on I→IV (18%).

  • Returns. A later verse keeps the first verse’s chords bar for bar 60% of the time, a chorus 55%, a pre-chorus 81%.

  • Loops. Under a strict reading (90% of bars repeat at the period), 29% of major choruses and 31% of verses loop in 1, 2, 4 or 8 bars, more in minor (47% of choruses); most sections vary their cell.

  • Over time. Harmony moved faster: the median verse changed chord every two bars in the 1950s and once a bar by the 1980s and 1990s.

A prototype chord writer, tested

Where the data is plentiful, chords drawn from kept sequences pass as kept; where it is thin, the model does no better than drawing chords at random by frequency.

The prototype is a variable-order Markov model (up to three chords of context) over each section kind’s chord changes, by mode, trained on 592 songs and read against the heldout 147. Each change’s length is drawn from the kind’s kept lengths. The baseline draws chords by their frequency alone. A first-chord or last-chord distance of 0 means the same distribution as the held-out songs, 1 means none in common.

Section Held out First chord: model / baseline Last chord: model / baseline Distinct chords: kept / model Tonic’s share: kept / model Bits a change: model / baseline
Verse, major 339 0.10/ 0.47 ** 0.14**/ 0.32 4 / 4 34% / 33% ** 3.81**/ 4.52
Chorus, major 329 ** 0.11**/ 0.32 ** 0.14**/ 0.31 4 / 4 29% / 29% ** 3.45**/ 4.44
Bridge, major 68 0.30 / 0.23 0.38 / 0.42 4 / 4 17% / 13% 3.98 / 4.79
Pre-chorus, major 38 0.28 / 0.35 0.46 / 0.74 4 / 4 0% / 10% 4.26 / 4.77
Verse, minor 68 0.15 / 0.50 0.26 / 0.33 3 / 4 49% / 32% 4.62 / 4.33
Chorus, minor 80 0.24 / 0.36 0.29 / 0.33 3 / 4 30% / 25% 4.40 / 4.38

Three things it shows:

  1. ** Verses and choruses in major pass.** First and last chords, distinct chords, the tonic’s share and changes a bar all sit with the held-out songs, and the model predicts the next chord about one bit better than frequency alone.

  2. Minor keys, bridges and pre-choruses do not. With 51 to 293 training sections, the model learns little beyond frequency (minor verses and choruses: no better in bits) and minor verses hold the tonic too little (32% against 49%). These need more songs, or rules from Table CP as gates.

  3. It rambles where real songs loop. Drawn sections run on (“I ii iii V vi iii IV I iii ii IV I”) where kept ones repeat a two-or four-bar cell. The writer needs a structural step: draw a cell, then repeat or vary it at the kept rates.

The same measures on a second collection

The section jobs hold on the Rolling Stone 200, a different collection analysed by different people: endings agree almost exactly, openings closely.

Section RS200 sections Last chord: RS200 / Billboard First chord: RS200 / Billboard Top moves, RS200
Verse 219 I 38%, V 29% / I 39%, V 29% I 69% / I 78% IV→I 13%, I→IV 12%, V→I 8%
Chorus 162 I 33%, V 27% / I 35%, V 28% I 43%, IV 15% / I 57%, IV 15% I→IV 13%, IV→I 11%, V→I 8%
Bridge 69 V 59% / V 46% I 29%, IV 23% / IV 34%, I 15% IV→I 10%, I→IV 8%
Intro 127 I 38%, V 22% / I 38%, V 22% I 64% / I 63% I→IV 11%, IV→I 11%

The RS200 figures mix major and minor songs (minor openings, i, take 12% to 15% of them), which accounts for most of the gap in first chords. Both collections change chord about once a bar and use three or four distinct chords a section.

What this means for v8’s harmony writer

Build it in three layers, chords first, and test it the way the melody is tested.

  1. The cell. For each section, draw a loop cell of one, two or four bars from the kept cells of its kind and mode, or decide not to loop, at the kept rates (about seven sections in ten in major do not repeat strictly).

  2. The sequence. Fill the cell, or the whole section when it does not loop, from the variable-order model of what follows what, by section kind and mode. Draw each chord’s length from the kind’s kept lengths, at the decade’s harmonic rhythm.

  3. The jobs as gates. Keep a section only if its first and last chords, distinct chords, tonic share and out-of-key share fall inside the kept quartiles (Tables CN and CP and these new tables). A verse opens on I; a pre-chorus or bridge ends off the tonic, usually on V; joins follow the kept moves.

Then the existing chart redraw (438) and the bass’s inversions (440) run unchanged, and the melody is drawn over the written chords, as it is now. Chords first suits the engine, whose melody search already aims at chord tones; the published systems that go melody first (MySong and its successors) remain a fallback if the fit test fails.

Mode and colour. Minor sections draw ♭VI, ♭VII, ♭III and Dorian’s IV at their kept rates; in major, ♭VII and II are the common outside chords. This replaces the hand-picked chord vocabularies by song type, which become a light preference on top.

What still needs data. Genre (country, soul, gospel, worship): the Billboard songs carry no labels, and the two labelled collections are unreachable here. Two routes: allow this session’s network to reach the Chordonomicon host (and settle its non-commercial licence for an engine that writes songs for release), or obtain de Clercq’s country corpus. Minor keys, bridges and pre-choruses: more songs, or the section-job gates carrying them until then.

The tests. (a) Every drawn section passes the gates; (b) on held-out songs the model beats frequency alone in bits a change for every kind it writes; (c) a listening check, drawn against kept progressions in the same section, as the published studies do; (d) the no-hand-input test from the audit: no chord in a song may come from a hand-typed list.

Files and sources

The scripts and tables are in the repo under studies/harmony (commit d8055b9): parse_billboard.py, measure_billboard.py (writes billboard_tables.json), prototype_writer.py (writes prototype_results.json) and rs200.py (writes rs200_tables.json). The raw data is ChoCo v1.0.0 from Zenodo record 7706751, not kept in the repo. This study follows the harmony and arrangement audit.

  • Harmony and Syntax in Contemporary Pop Music (Vassar thesis)

  • Chander and Quinn, The Decline of Harmonic Schemata in Popular Music Chord Loops (2023)

  • Park, Diatonic Chord Loops in Post-Millennial Pop Harmony (dissertation, 2025) On genre labels and corpus averages (Empirical Musicology Review) de Clercq, A Corpus Analysis of Harmony in Country Music (talk, 2021)

Part 19. Arrangement research for Luthier v8: what the band does

16 November 2026. Words and music: Jason Bryant. The live copy is the Doc Arrangement research for Luthier v8: what the band does.

A band writer for the engine can be drawn from measured parts the same way the melody is: real songs thin the intro to about 70% of their fullest band, thicken the chorus by about a quarter, play bass about once a beat at any tempo, and keep one drum bar for about half the song. Genre changes the details more than the plan.

The data

Two collections were measured; three more that would answer the open questions could not be reached from this session.

Collection What it is Used for Licence
POP909 with phrase labels 909 Chinese pop songs, each a lead melody, a secondary melody and a professional piano accompaniment; human phrase labels (intro, sung phrases A, B and others, instrumental phrases, outro) from Dai, Zhang and Dannenberg Texture by section role CC BY 4.0
Lakh Pianoroll Dataset (LPD-5 cleansed) 21,425 songs in five parts (drums, piano, guitar, bass, strings), 4/4; 9,329 read, up to 1,500 per genre The band by genre CC BY 4.0
Million Song Dataset artist terms (via Gold-Caps) Genre words per artist The genre of each Lakh song: the family of the artist’s top term CC BY 4.0
Groove MIDI Dataset Human drumming with fills labelled, many styles Not reached (its host is refused) CC BY 4.0
Timbre in Popular Song (TiPS) Texture and timbre by section in 400 songs, 100 each of country, pop, metal and hip hop Not reached; only the pop part is published so far Open, in progress
Lakh MIDI (raw) The original files with every instrument Not reached (its host is refused); the piano-roll version above stands in CC BY 4.0

Two limits run through the Lakh readings: piano rolls merge repeated strikes of one held pitch, so chordal onsets are undercounted; and genre comes from the artist, not the song.

The academic record

Scholars describe a pop band as a few functional layers whose entrances and exits mark the form; the most successful accompaniment systems retrieve real phrases and fit them to new chords rather than inventing parts note by note.

  • Functional layers (Moore 2012; Lavengood 2020). Allan Moore’s four layers: the explicit beat, the functional bass, the harmonic filler and the melody. Open Music Theory The harmonic filler fills the registral space between the bass and the melody. Lavengood, MTO Lavengood adds a fifth, the novelty layer: intermittent, cutting in and out, often in call and response with the melody, the least common of the layers. slides

  • The bass as its own line (de Clercq 2019). Bass and harmony often move independently, a “harmonic-bass divorce” like the melody’s. article

  • Form by texture. Most work on pop form has centred on melody and harmony, with drums studied less (Geary 2024). MTO Texture change cues listeners that a section is changing; the verse-to-chorus step intensifies by layering new instruments and changing register, and a late surge of new events often comes about two-thirds of the way through (Schwitzgebel 2025, after White 2020). MTO A 665-song McGill subset annotated for texture and timbre found these changes are formal markers, though only general trends were identified. McGill

  • Accumulation (Spicer 2004). “Accumulative” and “cumulative” forms build by layering, tied to multitrack recording. chapter

  • Genre norms are still being measured. TiPS encodes texture by section in 400 songs from the 1990s across country, pop, metal and hip hop on Moore’s and Lavengood’s layers. project Its genre norms have not been published yet.

  • Accompaniment systems. POP909 offers several professional piano arrangements of 909 popular songs for arrangement research. paper AccoMontage arranges whole pieces by retrieving phrase montages from a database, recombining them by dynamic programming, then fitting their chords to the lead sheet, and beat learning-only baselines. paper PopMAG generates multi-track accompaniment for pop. summary

Texture by section role (POP909)

The accompaniment lifts the chorus by playing more notes, not by moving register or holding longer: in 88% of songs the B phrases (usually the chorus) are busier than the A phrases (usually the verse).

Medians over 11,431 labelled phrases in 909 songs (piano accompaniment).

Phrase Phrases Piano notes a beat Notes struck together Share of time sounding Secondary melody, notes a beat
Intro 872 2.31 1.42 86% 1.24
A (usually the verse) 2,936 2.93 1.51 92% 0.50
B (usually the chorus) 3,074 3.78 1.65 92% 0.63
Other sung phrases 2,199 3.56 1.67 91% 0.69
Instrumental phrases 1,799 3.25 1.65 92% 1.36
Outro 551 2.82 1.41 91% 1.06

Paired within each song (865 songs with both): B over A by a median 0.73 notes a beat; B strikes more notes together in 61% of songs; register and sustain barely move (each about half up, half down). The secondary melody plays hardest where the voice rests: in intros and instrumental phrases at about twice its rate under sung phrases. The last chorus is no busier than the first (a median change of 0; busier in 49% of songs), unlike the build Window 38 reads in Western records; POP909’s piano-only Chinese pop may simply not build by the piano.

The band by genre (Lakh)

The plan is shared across genres and the details are not: every genre’s commonest snare bar is 2 and 4 and in every genre shown the intro starts at about 70% of the fullest band, while kick patterns, bass syncopation and piano voicing tell country from soul.

Medians unless noted; kick bars on sixteenths, x an onset, the bar starting left.

Genre Songs Snare on 2 and 4 (commonest bar) Commonest kick bars Hats in eighths / sixteenths Bass notes a beat Bass off the eighths Piano notes struck together Intro’s band, share of the fullest
Country 805 69% x.......x....... 28%, x.....x.x....... 23% 63% / 11% 0.91 1% 1.90 72%
Soul and R&B 598 67% x...x...x...x... 15%, x.......x....... 13% 61% / 17% 1.12 11% 2.16 70%
Gospel 274 62% x.......x....... 18%, x.....x.x....... 18% 59% / 12% 0.92 3% 1.83 69%
Pop 1,495 62% x...x...x...x... 20%, x.....x.x....... 16% 54% / 16% 1.05 5% 1.89 68%
Rock 1,498 64% x.....x.x....... 14%, x.......x....... 13% 57% / 13% 1.10 2% 1.92 67%
Folk 620 59% x.......x....... 14%, x.....x.x....... 14% 56% / 12% 0.98 3% 1.84 70%

What else the songs show:

One drum bar for half the song. The commonest kick-and-snare bar fills about half a song’s bars (country 61%, the steadiest; rock 48%), around ten other bars filling the rest.

  • The bass plays about once a beat at any tempo. Across all genres, a median 1.0 bass notes a beat below 85 beats a minute and 1.15 above 130. Slow songs syncopate more: 16% of bass notes fall off the eighths below 85, against 0.3% above 130. The engine’s routine, run at song 56’s doubled tempo, would have played about three a beat.

  • Chordal parts. Guitar’s commonest bar is straight eighths (15% to 23% of songs by genre). Country piano most often holds one chord a bar (20%), soul’s plays eighths (15%) with thicker chords. Strings hold whole notes. About half of chordal strikes land with a bass note (country guitar 44%, soul piano 57%). Registers, 10th to 90th percentile medians: piano about D3 to B4, guitar D3 to G4, strings A3 to F5.

  • Parts playing. The first tenth of a song carries about 70% of its fullest band, the second to the ninth tenths at the fullest, the last tenth about 93%. Drums enter by bar 1 or 2 in most songs.

  • What could not be read. Where fills fall: Lakh songs do not start on a phrase boundary, so fills by place in an 8-bar group come out flat. That needs phrase-aligned drums (the Groove MIDI Dataset labels its fills) or section labels.

What this means for v8’s arrangement writer

Build it the way AccoMontage and the melody search both work: draw real parts from kept tables, fit them to the engine’s chords, and gate them by the section plan.

  1. The section plan first. For each section, draw the number of parts and each part’s density from kept rates: the intro about 70% of the fullest band and its accompaniment about 0.6 times the chorus’s notes a beat; the chorus about a quarter busier than its verse; the middle of the song at the fullest; the last tenth about 93%. This writes Module 9.12’s arc and fix 238’s parts playing instead of hand-arranging to pass them.

  2. The four layers, one writer each (Moore’s layers, as in the spec’s Window 118 and 119 rules):

    • Explicit beat: the genre’s kick-and-snare bar drawn from the kept bars, held for about half the song with about ten variants; hats or ride in eighths unless the genre’s sixteenth rate is drawn; a fill and crash at section ends (fix 236).

    • Functional bass: the routine, made tempo-aware: about one note a beat at any tempo, syncopating off the eighths at the slow-song rate (16% below 85 beats a minute) and the genre’s rate (soul 11%, country 1%).

    • Harmonic filler: one bar rhythm per section from the instrument’s and genre’s kept rhythms (fix 237), voiced on the engine’s chords inside Module 9.14’s limits and the kept registers, about half of its strikes with the bass.

    • Novelty: short answers only in the voice’s gaps (the kept fill table, fix 235), intermittent, call and response, never under a sung word (fix 379).

  3. Tests. Every drawn arrangement passes 9.12, 9.13, 9.14, 236 to 238 and 379; its readings fall inside the genre’s kept quartiles above; and the no-hand-input test: no band note from a hand-typed list.

What still needs data. Fill placement and fill shapes by style (the Groove MIDI Dataset, refused here); texture by section in Western multitrack records (POP909 is Chinese pop and piano only; TiPS’s country, metal and hip-hop parts are unpublished); song-level genre labels (Lakh’s are the artist’s). Allowing this session’s network to reach those hosts, or attaching the files, would close most of it.

Files and sources

The scripts and tables are in the repo under studies/arrangement (commit 36a16ef): pop909_texture.py, lpd_genres.py, lpd_band.py and lpd_band_tables.py, with their JSON outputs. The raw data is not kept in the repo. This study follows the audit and the harmony study.

  • Geary, drums and form (MTO 2024)
